
Input:
You are an accomplished researcher in machine learning. You are considering a new method described in "New Method" for the research theme provided in "Research Topic". "Related Works" is a list of research papers that are highly relevant to this new method.
Based on the following instructions, output the reasons for the novelty and significance of the newly proposed method, and quantitatively evaluate them.

# Research Topic
Improving efficiency of hyperparameter optimization

# New Method
{
    "Open Problems": "Multi-fidelity HPO methods that exploit learning-curves (e.g. DyHPO, FastBO) fit a surrogate that jointly models configuration and budget.  Because the surrogate is learned purely by maximising (log-)marginal likelihood, the predicted mean curves are often non-monotone w.r.t. the budget, especially when only a few noisy points are available.  Non-monotone predictions mislead the acquisition function and waste evaluations on obviously inferior budgets.",
    "Methods": "We add a simple monotonicity regulariser to the surrogate training objective.\n1. Let m(x,b;θ) be the surrogate mean for configuration x at budget b.\n2. For every pair of observations (x,b_i),(x,b_j) with b_i<b_j in the training set build a penalty term\n   L_mono(θ)=∑_{(x,b_i<b_j)} ReLU(m(x,b_i;θ)-m(x,b_j;θ))^2.\n3. The new training loss becomes\n      L(θ)= -log p(y|θ)  + λ·L_mono(θ) ,   λ>0.\n   (λ≈0.1 works well and is the only extra hyper-parameter.)\n4. Everything else – kernel, deep feature extractor, acquisition function (EIMF) – is left unchanged.  The extra term is differentiable so DyHPO’s Adam optimiser needs only one extra backward call.\n\nIntuition: the hinge-squared penalty softly enforces m(x,b) to be non-decreasing in b, reflecting the fact that additional training never hurts in expectation.  This yields smoother, more realistic curves and makes the acquisition function focus on budgets that can still improve.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Base method   : DyHPO (official implementation).\nDatasets      : 5 LCBench neural–network tasks where DyHPO’s overhead was reported to outweigh its benefit (training time per epoch <0.2 s).\nBudgets       : Original 1…50 epochs.\nBaselines     : (a) Random Search (RS), (b) DyHPO, (c) DyHPO+Mono (ours).\nMetric        : Anytime regret (lower is better) vs wall-clock time; RMSE between predicted and true learning curves on a 20 % validation split.\nProtocol      : 10 independent runs, 256 total budgeted evaluations each; identical seeds across methods.\nλ-sweep       : try λ∈{0,0.03,0.1,0.3}; report best.\nHardware      : single RTX-2080 Ti; run time <2 hours.",
    "Experimental Code": "# --- key patch for DyHPO surrogate -----------------------------------------\nclass DyHPO(nn.Module):\n    ...\n    def loss(self, X,budget,curve, y):\n        \"\"\"compute -log marginal likelihood (mll) + monotone penalty\"\"\"\n        output = self.gp(X,budget,curve)           # unchanged\n        nll = -self.mll(output, y)\n        # --------- monotonicity penalty ---------\n        m_pred = output.mean                         # (N,)\n        b = budget.squeeze()                          # budgets used\n        # build pairwise indices with b_i<b_j\n        idx_i, idx_j = torch.triu_indices(len(b), len(b), offset=1)\n        mask = b[idx_i] < b[idx_j]\n        diff = m_pred[idx_i[mask]] - m_pred[idx_j[mask]]\n        mono_pen = torch.relu(diff).pow(2).mean()\n        return nll + self.lam * mono_pen\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# training loop   (same as DyHPO)\nmodel.lam = 0.1   # one line to activate regulariser",
    "Expected Result": "1. Learning-curve RMSE ↓ 15-25 % compared to vanilla DyHPO on all five tasks.\n2. Anytime regret: DyHPO+Mono reaches the final performance of vanilla DyHPO with ≈35 % less wall-clock time and beats Random Search throughout.\n3. With λ∈[0.03,0.3] results are stable; λ=0 reproduces vanilla DyHPO.\n4. Training overhead is <3 % (extra backward pass).",
    "Expected Conclusion": "A tiny, architecture-agnostic hinge penalty that encourages surrogate means to be non-decreasing in the budget removes unrealistically falling learning-curve predictions.  The acquisition function can therefore spend fewer evaluations on obviously saturated budgets, giving faster convergence at negligible computational cost.  Because the change touches only the loss function, it can be dropped into any learning-curve–aware BO method (FastBO, BOIL, etc.) with minimal engineering effort."
}

    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability limitations of First-Order Bayesian Optimization (FOBO) in high dimensions, where incorporating gradient information leads to computationally expensive kernel matrix operations. The main contributions include: 1) deriving analytical block-data-sparse structures for gradient kernel matrices, achieving an exact O(n^2d) matrix-vector multiplication (MVM) complexity; 2) proposing an Automatic Differentiation (AD) framework that programmatically computes these data-sparse block structures for various kernel transformations and combinations; 3) deriving analogous structures for kernel matrices arising from Hessian observations, reducing MVM complexity from O(n^2d^4) to O(n^2d^2); 4) providing numerical experiments demonstrating improved scaling and problem applicability; 5) comparing the proposed methods against existing techniques; and 6) utilizing the methods for Bayesian Optimization, enabling flexible, problem-dependent modeling while scaling first-order BO to high dimensions.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves exploiting the structured nature of kernel matrices when derivative information (gradients and Hessians) is incorporated into Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate models. The authors focus on the matrix-valued operator G = ∇x∇⊤y acting on kernel functions k(x,y) and show that G[k] is highly structured and data-sparse for a vast space of kernel functions. They derive rules for various kernel types and transformations: for kernels expressed as f◦g, G[f◦g] is a rank-one correction to G[g], inheriting its O(d) MVM property. For sums and products of kernels, they show that MVM complexity scales as O(dr) or O(dr) for rank-r corrections, respectively. For direct sums and products of separable kernels, MVM is O(d). Vertical rescaling (f(x)h(x,y)f(y)) and warping (h(u(x),u(y))) also yield low-rank corrections or factorable Jacobian terms, leading to O(n^2d) MVM complexity for gradients. The paper extends these structured representations to complex composite kernels (e.g., neural network, RBF-network, spectral mixture kernels) without additional derivations. For Hessian observations, structured representations are derived for isotropic kernels, achieving O(n^2d^2) MVM. An implementation, CovarianceFunctions.jl in Julia, uses user-defined types and lazy evaluation to achieve O(1) memory complexity for MVM, integrating with ForwardDiff.jl for automatic computation of derivatives. For Bayesian Optimization, a composite kernel f = g+h is proposed, where g is a quadratic function (modeled by (x·y+c)^2 kernel) and h is a non-convex function (Matérn-5/2 kernel), to exploit inductive biases for problems with a globally quadratic structure.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes: 1) **Scaling on Synthetic Data:** Benchmarking matrix-vector multiplications for gradient and Hessian kernel matrices using non-separable rational quadratic, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels. Scaling plots were generated with a single thread, while heat maps showing execution time were run with 24 threads on 12 cores to assess applicability on modern parallel architectures. 2) **Comparison to Prior Work:** The proposed method was compared against popular libraries like GPyTorch, GPFlow, and Scikit-Learn (which generally lack efficient gradient observation inference), specifically showing GPyTorch's RBFKernelGrad and PolynomialKernelGrad (O(n^2d^2) naïve complexity) are outperformed. It was also compared to D-SKIP (Eriksson et al., 2018), an approximate method, in terms of MVM cost and accuracy for RBF gradient kernel matrices. 3) **Bayesian Optimization:** Optimization algorithms were benchmarked on three non-convex test functions: Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin. Input domains were scaled to [-1,1]^d, outputs to [0,1], and global optima shifted to 1d/4. Experiments were conducted in 4D and 16D. Strategies compared include random sampling, L-BFGS, L-BFGS with random restarts (L-BFGS-R), standard BO, BO with the quadratic mixture kernel (BO-Q), FOBO, and FOBO with the quadratic mixture kernel (FOBO-Q). All BO variants used the expected improvement acquisition function, numerically optimized using L-BFGS with a random restart mechanism if the proposed next observation was too close to a previous one (within 10^-4). Results were averaged over 128 independent experiments to report optimality gap.",
    "Limitations": "The limitations acknowledged by the authors include that global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard and cannot be generally solved. For instance, the 16D Ackley function's optimum was elusive for all methods, likely due to its exponentially shrinking domain of attraction with increasing dimensionality. Although structured representations for Hessian observations were derived, the primary focus of the experimental evaluation and BO application was on first-order information. A comprehensive comparison of first and second-order BO was explicitly left for future work. Furthermore, while the proposed methods achieve O(d) MVM complexity, the O(n^2) factor can still be expensive for a very large number of observations (n).",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper suggests several future research directions: 1) linking the proposed structured AD technology with Maclaurin et al. (2015)'s algorithm for computing hyper-parameter gradients, which would enable efficient FOBO of hyper-parameters; 2) exploring analysis-based fast algorithms, such as Ryan et al. (2022)'s Fast Kernel Transform, for gradient kernels, especially in low dimensions, to address the O(n^2) scaling for large n; 3) exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, particularly when converging to a minimum, potentially using sparse linear solvers; and 4) utilizing Hessian observations for Bayesian Quadrature, given the established benefits of second-order information for integration (e.g., Laplace approximation), along with a more comprehensive comparison of first-order and second-order BO.",
    "Experiment Code": null,

    "Title": "Scalable First-Order Bayesian Optimization via Structured Automatic Differentiation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the scalability limitations of First-Order Bayesian Optimization (FOBO) in high dimensions, where incorporating gradient information into Gaussian Process (GP) surrogates leads to computationally expensive kernel matrix operations (O(n^2d^2) for multiply, O(n^3d^3) for inversion). The main contributions include deriving analytical block-data-sparse structures for gradient kernel matrices, enabling an exact O(n^2d) matrix-vector multiply. A structure-aware automatic differentiation (AD) framework is proposed to programmatically compute these data-sparse structures for a wide range of kernel transformations and combinations, including complex composite kernels like neural network and spectral mixture kernels. The work also extends these derivations to Hessian kernel matrices, reducing their computational complexity from O(n^2d^4) to O(n^2d^2). Numerical experiments demonstrate improved scaling, and the methods are applied to Bayesian Optimization, showing enhanced performance on test functions.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology is based on leveraging structured automatic differentiation for Gaussian Process covariance kernels. It focuses on the matrix-valued operator G = ∇x∇⊤y acting on kernel functions. The paper derives novel structured representations of G[k] for various kernel input types (isotropic, stationary linear functional, dot product) and transformations (chain rule, sums, products, direct sums/products, vertical rescaling, and warping). This structure allows for O(d) or O(d^2) complexity for matrix-vector multiplications with G[k] elements. For Hessian information, analogous structured representations are derived for Hessian-Hessian covariance and gradient-Hessian cross-covariance, reducing complexity to O(n^2d^2). The proposed structure-aware AD system, implemented in `CovarianceFunctions.jl` (Julia), programmatically identifies and exploits these block-data-sparse structures, using user-defined types for kernels and `ForwardDiff.jl` for general derivatives. It employs lazy evaluation for O(1) memory complexity, making it scalable to large numbers of observations. For Bayesian Optimization, a quadratic mixture kernel (Matérn-5/2 + (x·y+c)²) is introduced to model objectives as a sum of a quadratic and a non-convex function, improving inductive bias.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup includes practical scaling studies on synthetic data using non-separable rational quadratic, exponentiated dot-product, and neural network kernels to demonstrate the O(d) scaling for gradient and O(d^2) for Hessian kernel matrix-vector multiplications. Benchmarks were conducted on a modern parallel architecture (24 threads on 12 cores for heatmaps, single thread for scaling plots). Comparisons were made against existing GP libraries (GPyTorch, GPFlow, Scikit-Learn, Dragonﬂy, BayesOpt) and the approximate D-SKIP method (Eriksson et al., 2018), evaluating both performance (MVM time) and accuracy (relative error for D-SKIP). For Bayesian Optimization, the methods were benchmarked on three non-convex test functions: Griewank, Ackley, and Rastrigin, with input domains scaled to [−1,1]^d and output to [0,1]. Optimization strategies compared included random sampling, L-BFGS, L-BFGS with random restarts (L-BFGS-R), standard BO, BO with the quadratic mixture kernel (BO-Q), FOBO, and FOBO with the quadratic mixture kernel (FOBO-Q). All BO variants used the expected improvement acquisition function, optimized via L-BFGS, and incorporated a restart mechanism for local minima. Experiments were averaged over 128 independent runs.",
    "Limitations": "The global optimization of non-convex functions remains NP-hard, and the proposed methods, while improving BO performance, cannot generally solve this. For instance, the optimum of the 16D Ackley function proved elusive for all tested methods, potentially due to its vanishing domain of attraction in high dimensions. Although structured representations for Hessian observations were derived and their computational scaling demonstrated, these were not explicitly used for Bayesian Optimization in this work. A comprehensive comparison of first and second-order BO is left for future research. The primary focus was on enabling such investigations by providing the theoretical advances and practical infrastructure.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include linking the proposed structured AD technology with algorithms for hyper-parameter gradient computation (e.g., Maclaurin et al., 2015) to enable efficient FOBO of hyper-parameters. For scenarios with a very large number of observations (n), where the O(n^2) complexity of the current exact methods can still be expensive, the development of analysis-based fast algorithms (like Ryan et al., 2022's Fast Kernel Transform) for gradient kernels holds promise, particularly in low dimensions. Another direction involves exploiting redundant information in BO trajectories, especially when converging to a minimum, using sparse linear solvers (e.g., Ament and Gomes, 2021). Finally, leveraging Hessian observations for Bayesian Quadrature is suggested, given the established benefits of second-order information for integration, such as in Laplace approximations for estimating integrals in Bayesian statistics.",
    "Experiment Code": null,

    "Title": "Efficient Nonmyopic Bayesian Optimization via One-Shot Multi-Step Trees",
    "Main Contributions": "This paper presents the first efficient implementation of general multi-step lookahead Bayesian Optimization (BO). It introduces \"one-shot multi-step trees,\" a novel scenario tree-based acquisition function that performs approximate multi-step lookahead by jointly optimizing all decision variables in a single, higher-dimensional problem, leveraging the reparameterization trick and auto-differentiation. The work also develops \"fast fantasies\"—efficient linear algebra methods for conditioning Gaussian Process (GP) models—combined with a highly parallelizable implementation on accelerated hardware for multi-step GP fantasization. The proposed method demonstrates superior optimization performance over one-step Expected Improvement (EI) and BINOCULARS on a wide range of benchmarks, while maintaining competitive wall times. Additionally, two scalable special cases, Multi-Step Path and Non-Adaptive Approximation (ENO), are identified, showing surprisingly good performance.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves approximating the intractable k-step BO problem using a differentiable, tree-based acquisition function. Nested expectations are handled via Monte Carlo integration, forming a discrete scenario tree. The reparameterization trick is applied recursively to express fantasy samples as deterministic functions of query locations and independent base samples, making the acquisition function differentiable. Instead of nested optimization, all decision variables (query points at various stages of the tree) are jointly optimized in a \"one-shot\" fashion using gradient-based methods (L-BFGS-B) with auto-differentiation and a warm-starting strategy. For computational efficiency, the paper introduces \"multi-step fast fantasies,\" a novel linear algebra method for efficiently updating GPyTorch's LOVE (Lanczos Variance Estimates) caches in O(nrq) time to condition GP models. This represents tree levels using batch dimensions in batched GP models, exploiting parallelization. Two scalable variants are proposed: Multi-Step Path (using only one fantasy sample per subsequent stage) and Non-Adaptive Approximation (ENO) which approximates future adaptive decisions with non-adaptive ones.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental setup follows that of previous work, using nine synthetic ",
    "Limitations": "The primary limitation is that the dimension of the general one-shot optimization problem grows exponentially with the lookahead horizon (k), practically limiting the method to small horizons (e.g., less than 5 steps). Empirical results show diminishing returns in optimization performance for horizons beyond 3 steps, which might be attributed to the increasing difficulty of optimizing complex multi-step objectives or an over-reliance on the accuracy of the surrogate model in deep lookahead. Additionally, previous two-step lookahead methods relied on strong assumptions about global optima of second-stage value functions, which if violated, could lead to discontinuities in the objective function.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A key future research direction is to investigate the application of the proposed efficient multi-step lookahead framework to other sequential experimental design problems beyond standard black-box optimization, such as Bayesian quadrature.",

    "Title": "A General Framework for User-Guided Bayesian Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the limited ability of Bayesian Optimization (BO) to incorporate diverse prior knowledge, reducing its appeal for knowledgeable practitioners. It proposes ColaBO, a Bayesian-principled framework that allows users to inject prior beliefs beyond typical kernel structures, such as the likely location of the optimizer, the optimal value, or preference relations. ColaBO is shown to accelerate optimization with accurate prior information and maintain default performance when priors are misleading. Key contributions include introducing ColaBO for incorporating user knowledge into BO as an additional prior orthogonal to kernel hyperparameters, demonstrating its general applicability to Monte Carlo acquisition functions, and empirically showing accelerated optimization with accurate priors over optimal location and value.",
    "Methodology": "ColaBO operates by introducing an augmented user belief over function properties, such as the optimum or optimal value, which reshapes the Gaussian Process (GP) prior to reflect these beliefs. It deviates from typical Gaussian posterior assumptions and uses rejection sampling on draws from the GP prior to form a 'belief-weighted prior' (p(f|ρ)). When observations (D) are obtained, the posterior (p(f|D, ρ)) is updated using a pathwise update (Equation 8) that preserves the shape of the belief-weighted samples far from observed data. This non-Gaussian, belief-weighted posterior is then used with Monte Carlo (MC) acquisition functions (e.g., Expected Improvement (EI), Max-Value Entropy Search (MES)) by re-sampling functions proportionally to their probability under the user belief. This biases the acquisition function to direct the search towards regions consistent with the prior.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The performance of ColaBO was evaluated on synthetic functions (Hartmann (3D, 4D, 6D), Levy (5D), Rosenbrock (6D), Stybtang (7D)) with known optima, using both well-located and poorly located priors over the optimal location and optimal value. Real-world hyperparameter optimization (HPO) tasks from LCBench (five 6D deep learning tasks) and PD1 (three 4D deep learning tasks) were also used, with priors obtained from MF-Prior-Bench. All methods utilized a squared exponential kernel (with Matern-5/2 kernel experiments in Appendix B.1), MAP hyperparameter estimation, and were implemented in BoTorch. Initialization involved the mode of the prior followed by 2 Sobol samples for synthetic tasks, and one initial sample (mode of prior) for HPO tasks. Benchmarking included vanilla acquisition functions (LogEI, MES), πBO, and decoupled Thompson sampling. Performance was measured by log regret and best observed value over iterations. Budget-related parameters for MC approximations (number of function samples, RFFs, resamples, and optimal values for MES) were specified.",
    "Limitations": "ColaBO relies heavily on multiple steps of Monte Carlo (MC) integration, leading to high computational expense (tens of seconds per evaluation for desired accuracy). Obtaining draws from user beliefs (e.g., ρ˚x) scales exponentially with the dimensionality of the prior, potentially making it impractical for high-dimensional priors beyond a handful of variables. Additionally, ColaBO cannot leverage Quasi-MC for posterior predictive draws (only for RFF weights) or fix base samples for acquisition function consistency across the search space. The Random Fourier Features (RFF) approximation introduces bias, which is more pronounced for the Matérn 5/2-kernel compared to the squared exponential kernel.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on developing more accurate and efficient sampling procedures from the belief-weighted prior. Exploring variational inference (Titsias, 2009) or pre-trained model approaches (Müller et al., 2022; Müller et al., 2023) could lead to a representative belief-biased model with an analytical posterior, potentially reducing ColaBO's runtime and broadening its applicability. Another promising avenue is to apply ColaBO to multi-fidelity optimization (Kandasamy et al., 2016; Mallik et al., 2023) to further enhance its efficiency for costly deep learning pipelines.",
    "Experiment Code": "The provided repository content (files from `Ax/ax/`) does not contain the specific implementation sections directly related to the ColaBO method, such as the augmented user belief mechanism, reshaping of the Gaussian Process (GP) prior to reflect beliefs, rejection sampling on draws from the GP prior to form a 'belief-weighted prior' (p(f|ρ)), or the pathwise update (Equation 8) for the posterior (p(f|D, ρ)). The files primarily detail the core Ax platform's architecture, benchmarking infrastructure, and integration with standard BoTorch models and acquisition functions (e.g., GPEI, MOO, SAASBO, qNoisyExpectedImprovement, qNoisyExpectedHypervolumeImprovement), but not the novel components of ColaBO.",
    "Experiment Result": "The repository content does not contain specific experimental settings or configurations directly for the ColaBO method.     "Title": "Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization via Deep Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The main research problem addressed is the inefficiency and inaccuracy of existing multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization (BO) methods in estimating objective functions due to their oversimplified modeling or neglect of complex correlations across fidelities. The paper proposes Deep Neural Network Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization (DNN-MFBO), which utilizes a stack of neural networks to flexibly capture highly nonlinear and nonstationary relationships between fidelities. Key contributions include: 1) A novel deep neural network surrogate model that propagates information across fidelities to improve objective function estimation. 2) A tractable and highly efficient method for calculating a mutual information-based acquisition function using sequential, fidelity-wise Gauss-Hermite quadrature and moment-matching. 3) Demonstrated superior optimization performance (lower simple and inference regrets, better queried optimums) with smaller or comparable query costs on synthetic benchmarks and real-world engineering design problems. 4) Achieved significantly faster acquisition function computation compared to multi-output GP-based baselines.",
    "Methodology": "DNN-MFBO models multi-fidelity functions using a stack of deep neural networks (NNs), where each NN corresponds to a fidelity. For fidelities `m > 1`, the NN's input is augmented with the output of the previous fidelity (`fm-1(x)`) to capture complex inter-fidelity relationships, while for `m=1`, it takes only the original input `x`. The NN output is defined as `fm(x) = w⊤mφθm(xm)`, where `wm` are output layer weights (random variables with a standard normal prior) and `θm` are other layer weights (hyperparameters). A stochastic variational learning algorithm is developed to jointly estimate the Gaussian posterior `q(wm)` and optimize `θm` and noise variances by maximizing an Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO) using the reparameterization trick. For optimization, a max-value entropy search (MES) acquisition function is used. To compute the acquisition function's posterior distributions `p(fm(x)|D)`, a critical challenge due to nonlinear fidelity coupling, the method employs sequential, fidelity-wise moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature to approximate each posterior as a Gaussian. The two entropy terms of the MES acquisition function are then calculated: `H(fm(x)|D)` is directly computed from the Gaussian approximation, and `Ep(f*|D)[H(fm(x)|f*, D)]` is approximated via Monte Carlo sampling of `f*` and further approximating `H(fm(x)|f*, D)` by `H(fm(x)|fM(x) ≤ f*, D)`. This approximation for `m < M` involves moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature on conditional posteriors. The acquisition function is maximized using L-BFGS with random initialization, with gradients computed via automatic differentiation.",
    "Experimental Setup": "DNN-MFBO was implemented using TensorFlow and compared against MF-SKO, MF-GP-UCB, MF-PES, MF-MES, MTNN-BO (also TensorFlow), and SF-MES (using their original implementations). For DNN-MFBO and MTNN-BO, ReLU activation was used, and NN architecture (depth [2,12], width [32,512]) and learning rates [10e-5, 10e-1] were tuned using SMAC3 and manual refinement. ADAM optimizer ran for 5,000 epochs. Acquisition functions were optimized with L-BFGS (random initialization for DNN-MFBO/MTNN-BO; global search followed by L-BFGS for others). Initial training points were randomly queried across fidelities (e.g., Branin/Levy: 20, 20, 2 samples for 3 fidelities; Park1: 5, 2 samples for 2 fidelities; real-world: 20, 5 samples for 2 fidelities). Query costs (`λm`) were set (e.g., (1, 10, 100) for 3 fidelities, (1, 10) for 2 fidelities). Evaluations were performed on three synthetic benchmark functions (Branin, Park1, Levy), and two real-world engineering design problems (Mechanical Plate Vibration Design and Thermal Conductor Design), all featuring complex inter-fidelity transformations. Metrics included simple regret, inference regret, best queried function values (for real-world), and average query time. Experiments were repeated five times, with averages and standard error bars reported. Query time comparisons utilized a Linux workstation with a 16-core Intel Xeon E5-2670 CPU and 16GB RAM.",
    "Limitations": "The current work explicitly focuses on discrete fidelities, not addressing continuous fidelity settings. The acquisition function calculation, despite being analytically tractable, is described as 'quite complex.' The use of `H(fm(x)|fM(x) ≤ f*, D)` as a 'reasonable approximation' for `H(fm(x)|max fM(x) = f*, D)` introduces a degree of inexactness in the entropy computation. The maximization of the acquisition function relies on L-BFGS with random initialization, which, while efficient, does not guarantee finding the global optimum of the acquisition function. Finally, the selection of neural network hyperparameters (depth, width, learning rate) involved an initial tuning phase with an AutoML tool (SMAC3) followed by manual refinement, suggesting that this aspect of the method is not fully automated or might require expert knowledge for new applications.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Not mentioned",

ret1)\n    ret2 = np.linalg.norm(np.array([gx2, gy2]) - ret2)\n    sys.stdout.write(str(ret1+ret2))\n\nFile: test_functions/python_related/push_world.py\nContent:\n(Relevant functions)\nclass b2WorldInterface:\n    def __init__(self, do_gui=True):\n        self.world = b2World(gravity=(0.0,0.0), doSleep=True)\n        self.do_gui = do_gui\n        self.TARGET_FPS = 100\n        self.TIME_STEP = 1.0/self.TARGET_FPS\n        self.VEL_ITERS, self.POS_ITERS =10,10\n        self.bodies = []\n\n        if do_gui:\n            self.gui_world  = guiWorld(self.TARGET_FPS)\n        else:\n            self.gui_world = None\n    def step(self, show_display=True, idx=0):\n        self.world.Step(self.TIME_STEP, self.VEL_ITERS, self.POS_ITERS)\n\nclass end_effector:\n    def __init__(self, b2world_interface, init_pos, base, init_angle, hand_shape='rectangle', hand_size=(0.3,1)):\n        world= b2world_interface.world\n        self.hand = world.CreateDynamicBody(position=init_pos,angle=init_angle)\n        self.hand_shape = hand_shape\n        self.hand_size = hand_size\n        if hand_shape == 'rectangle':\n            rshape = b2PolygonShape(box=hand_size)\n            self.forceunit = 30.0\n        elif hand_shape == 'circle':\n            rshape = b2CircleShape(radius=hand_size)\n            self.forceunit = 100.0\n        self.hand.CreateFixture(\n            shape = rshape,\n            density = .1,\n            friction = .1\n            )\n        self.hand.userData = \"hand\"\n        friction_joint = world.CreateFrictionJoint(\n            bodyA = base,\n            bodyB = self.hand,\n            maxForce = 2,\n            maxTorque = 2,\n            )\n        b2world_interface.add_bodies(self.hand)\n\n    def apply_wrench(self, rlvel=(0,0), ravel=0):\n        avel = self.hand.angularVelocity\n        delta_avel = ravel - avel\n        torque = self.hand.mass*delta_avel*30.0\n        self.hand.ApplyTorque(torque, wake=True)\n        \n        lvel = self.hand.linearVelocity\n        delta_lvel = b2Vec2(rlvel) - b2Vec2(lvel)\n        force = self.hand.mass*delta_lvel*self.forceunit\n        self.hand.ApplyForce(force, self.hand.position,wake=True)\n\ndef make_thing(table_width, table_length, b2world_interface, thing_shape, thing_size, thing_friction, thing_density, base_friction, obj_loc):\n    world = b2world_interface.world\n    base = world.CreateStaticBody(\n            position = (0,0),\n            shapes = b2PolygonShape(box=(table_length,table_width)),\n            )\n\n    link = world.CreateDynamicBody(position=obj_loc)\n    if thing_shape == 'rectangle':\n        linkshape = b2PolygonShape(box=thing_size)\n    elif thing_shape == 'circle':\n        linkshape = b2CircleShape(radius=thing_size)\n    link.CreateFixture(\n            shape = linkshape, \n            density = thing_density,\n            friction = thing_friction,\n            )\n    friction_joint = world.CreateFrictionJoint(\n            bodyA = base,\n            bodyB = link,\n            maxForce = 5,\n            maxTorque = 2,\n            )\n\n    b2world_interface.add_bodies([base,link])\n    return link,base\n\ndef simu_push(world, thing, robot, base, simulation_steps):\n    desired_vel = thing.position - robot.hand.position\n    desired_vel = desired_vel / np.linalg.norm(desired_vel) * 5\n    rvel = b2Vec2(desired_vel[0]+np.random.normal(0,0.1),desired_vel[1]+np.random.normal(0,0.1))\n\n    rstop = False\n    for t in range(simulation_steps+100):\n        if not rstop:\n            robot.apply_wrench(rvel)\n        world.step()\n        if t == simulation_steps - 1:\n            rstop = True\n    return list(thing.position)\n\ndef simu_push2(world, thing, robot, base, xvel, yvel, simulation_steps):\n    desired_vel = np.array([xvel, yvel])\n    rvel = b2Vec2(desired_vel[0]+np.random.normal(0,0.1),desired_vel[1]+np.random.normal(0,0.1))\n\n    rstop = False\n    for t in range(simulation_steps+100):\n        if not rstop:\n            robot.apply_wrench(rvel)\n        world.step()\n        if t == simulation_steps - 1:\n            rstop = True\n    return list(thing.position)\n\ndef make_1thing(base, b2world_interface, thing_shape, thing_size, thing_friction, thing_density, obj_loc):\n    world = b2world_interface.world\n    link = world.CreateDynamicBody(position=obj_loc)\n    if thing_shape == 'rectangle':\n        linkshape = b2PolygonShape(box=thing_size)\n    elif thing_shape == 'circle':\n        linkshape = b2CircleShape(radius=thing_size)\n    link.CreateFixture(\n            shape = linkshape, \n            density = thing_density,\n            friction = thing_friction,\n            )\n    friction_joint = world.CreateFrictionJoint(\n            bodyA = base,\n            bodyB = link,\n            maxForce = 5,\n            maxTorque = 2,\n            )\n\n    b2world_interface.add_bodies([link])\n    return link\n\ndef simu_push_2robot2thing(world, thing, thing2, robot, robot2, base, xvel, yvel, xvel2, yvel2, rtor, rtor2, simulation_steps, simulation_steps2):\n    desired_vel = np.array([xvel, yvel])\n    rvel = b2Vec2(desired_vel[0]+np.random.normal(0,0.01),desired_vel[1]+np.random.normal(0,0.01))\n    \n    desired_vel2 = np.array([xvel2, yvel2])\n    rvel2 = b2Vec2(desired_vel2[0]+np.random.normal(0,0.01),desired_vel2[1]+np.random.normal(0,0.01))\n    tmax = np.max([simulation_steps,simulation_steps2])\n    for t in range(tmax+100):\n        if t < simulation_steps:\n            robot.apply_wrench(rvel, rtor)\n        if t < simulation_steps2:\n            robot2.apply_wrench(rvel2, rtor2)\n        world.step()\n    return (list(thing.position), list(thing2.position))\n\ndef make_base(table_width, table_length, b2world_interface):\n    world = b2world_interface.world\n    base = world.CreateStaticBody(\n            position = (0,0),\n            shapes = b2PolygonShape(box=(table_length,table_width)),\n            )\n    b2world_interface.add_bodies([base])\n    return base\n",
    "Experiment Result": "The repository provides Python scripts for simulating robotic pushing tasks using the Box2D physics engine, which can serve as objective functions for Bayesian Optimization methods like DNN-MFBO. The experimental settings are as follows:\n\n**General Simulation Environment:**\n-   **Physics Engine:** Box2D (via `pybox2d` library).\n-   **Gravity:** (0.0, 0.0).\n-   **GUI:** Disabled by default (`b2WorldInterface(False)`).\n-   **Time Stepping:** `TARGET_FPS = 100`, `TIME_STEP = 1.0 / TARGET_FPS` (0.01 seconds per step). `VEL_ITERS = 10`, `POS_ITERS = 10` for world step.\n-   **Base/Table:** A static body created with `make_thing` or `make_base`, typically (0,0) position, 500x500 units in `generate_simudata` scripts.\n\n**Object (Thing) Properties (for `make_thing`/`make_1thing`):**\n-   **Shape:** `circle` or `rectangle` (or `polygon`). Default: `circle`.\n-   **Size:** `osize=1` (radius for circle) or `(0.5,0.5)` for rectangle.\n-   **Friction:** `ofriction=0.01`.\n-   **Density:** `odensity=0.05`.\n-   **Initial Position:** Varies, e.g., (0,0) in `generate_simudata3/4`, (0,2) or (0,-2) in `generate_simudata_2robot2thing`.\n-   **Friction Joint (Object-Base):** `maxForce = 5`, `maxTorque = 2`.\n\n**Robot (End-Effector) Properties:**\n-   **Hand Shape:** `rectangle` or `circle`. Default: `rectangle`.\n-   **Hand Size:** `(0.3,1)` for `generate_simudata3/4`, `(1,0.3)` for `generate_simudata_2robot2thing`.\n-   **Density:** `0.1`.\n-   **Friction:** `0.1`.\n-   **Force Unit:** `30.0` for rectangle hand, `100.0` for circle hand, used in `apply_wrench` to calculate force from desired velocity.\n-   **Friction Joint (Robot-Base):** `maxForce = 2`, `maxTorque = 2`.\n\n**Task Objective:**\n-   The objective is to minimize the Euclidean distance between the final position of the pushed object(s) (`ret` or `ret1`, `ret2`) and a predefined goal position (`gx, gy` or `gx2, gy2`). For the multi-robot scenario, the sum of distances for multiple objects is minimized.\n\n**Specific Simulation Scenarios and Input Parameters (`sys.argv` arguments):**\n1.  **`generate_simudata3.py` (Single Robot, Single Object):**\n    -   **Inputs:** `rx` (robot initial x), `ry` (robot initial y), `simu_steps` (simulation duration, multiplied by 10), `gx` (goal x), `gy` (goal y).\n    -   **Robot Motion:** The robot pushes with a fixed direction, pointing from its initial location (`rx, ry`) towards the object's initial location, with a speed of 5 units. A small Gaussian noise (mean 0, std dev 0.1) is added to the desired velocity.\n    -   `init_angle` for robot is derived from `arctan(ry/rx)`.\n\n2.  **`generate_simudata4.py` (Single Robot, Single Object with Angle Control):**\n    -   **Inputs:** `rx`, `ry`, `simu_steps` (multiplied by 10), `gx`, `gy`, `init_angle` (robot initial angle).\n    -   **Robot Motion:** The robot's desired linear velocity (`xvel`, `yvel`) is derived from `-rx, -ry` and then normalized and scaled to 10 units. A small Gaussian noise (mean 0, std dev 0.1) is added to the desired velocity.\n\n3.  **`generate_simudata_2robot2thing.py` (Two Robots, Two Objects):**\n    -   **Inputs (18 arguments):** `rx`, `ry`, `xvel`, `yvel`, `simu_steps`, `init_angle` for robot 1, and similarly `rx2`, `ry2`, `xvel2`, `yvel2`, `simu_steps2`, `init_angle2` for robot 2. Additionally, `rtor`, `rtor2` (robot angular velocities/torques), and `gx`, `gy`, `gx2`, `gy2` (goal positions for thing 1 and thing 2).\n    -   **Objects:** One `rectangle` object at `(0, 2)` and one `circle` object at `(0, -2)`.\n    -   **Robot Motion:** Two robots push two separate objects. Each robot applies a wrench based on specified linear velocities (`xvel`, `yvel`) and angular velocities (`rtor`). A small Gaussian noise (mean 0, std dev 0.01) is added to the desired linear velocity for both robots.\n    -   `tmax` is the maximum of `simulation_steps` and `simulation_steps2`, determining overall simulation duration.\n\n**Fidelity Relation to DNN-MFBO:**\nThe `simu_steps` (and `simu_steps2`) parameters in these scripts could be interpreted as fidelity levels for a multi-fidelity optimization task. A higher `simu_steps` value would correspond to a higher-fidelity (more computationally expensive) simulation, providing a more precise evaluation of the objective function, as described in the DNN-MFBO method where different NNs correspond to different fidelities."

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "FastBO proposes a multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization method to adaptively determine the appropriate fidelity for each hyperparameter configuration to fit the surrogate model, addressing the challenge of intersecting learning curves in HPO and NAS. This is achieved through the novel concepts of \"efficient point\" and \"saturation point\", enabling efficient high-quality performance. The proposed adaptive fidelity identification strategy also provides a general way to extend any single-fidelity method to the multi-fidelity setting.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO introduces two key concepts: the \"efficient point\" (e_i), defined as the resource level beyond which doubling resources yields minimal performance improvement, and the \"saturation point\" (s_i), where a configuration's performance stabilizes. The FastBO process involves a warm-up stage for initial observations and termination of poor configurations, followed by learning curve estimation to extract efficient and saturation points. Configurations are then evaluated up to their efficient points to update the surrogate model. Finally, a post-processing stage evaluates a small set of promising configurations to their saturation points to identify the optimal configuration. This adaptive strategy can generalize to extend single-fidelity methods by utilizing efficient point performances.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FastBO was evaluated and compared against several state-of-the-art methods, including Random Search (RS), standard BO, ASHA, Hyperband, PASHA, A-BOHB, A-CQR, BOHB, DyHPO, and Hyper-Tune. The experiments were conducted on three established benchmarks: LCBench, NAS-Bench-201, and FCNet. The validation focused on anytime performance, demonstrating FastBO's ability to gain an advantage earlier and rapidly converge to the global optimum compared to other methods.",
    "Limitations": "The paper implies current limitations in applicability and scalability, as it suggests that future work should refine and expand FastBO to larger search spaces and distributed computing systems.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research should focus on refining and expanding FastBO to effectively operate in larger search spaces and distributed computing systems. This expansion aims to significantly improve the method's applicability and scalability in more complex and resource-intensive environments.",

    "Title": "Batch Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization with  Deep Auto-Regressive Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Batch Multi-fidelity Bayesian Optimization with Deep Auto-Regressive Networks (BMBO-DARN) for optimizing expensive black-box functions. Its main contributions include a deep auto-regressive model that uses Bayesian neural networks to capture strong, complex (nonlinear, nonstationary) relationships across different fidelities, significantly improving surrogate learning. It also develops an efficient batch querying method based on the Max-value Entropy Search (MES) principle, designed to penalize highly correlated queries and encourage diversity, thereby enhancing query quality. For efficient computation, it employs posterior samples and moment matching to approximate the acquisition function, and an alternating optimization algorithm to jointly identify optimal inputs and fidelities without resorting to costly combinatorial search. The approach is empirically demonstrated to yield superior performance and cost efficiency in four real-world hyperparameter optimization applications.",
    "Methodology": "BMBO-DARN employs a deep auto-regressive model consisting of a chain of M Bayesian Neural Networks (BNNs), with one BNN for each fidelity. The input for the m-th fidelity's BNN (`xm`) includes the original objective function input `x` and the outputs of all preceding fidelities (`f1(x), ..., fm-1(x)`), enabling the model to learn complex inter-fidelity relationships. Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) sampling is utilized for posterior inference of the BNN weights, providing robust uncertainty quantification. A novel batch acquisition function is introduced, formulated as the mutual information between a batch of queried function values and the function optimum, divided by the total querying cost, thus balancing exploration benefit and expense. To efficiently compute this acquisition function, posterior samples of BNN weights are used to estimate the first and second moments, matching them to a multivariate Gaussian distribution for closed-form mutual information calculation. Maximizing this acquisition function to determine the next batch of input-fidelity pairs is achieved through an alternating optimization algorithm, where each input-fidelity pair is optimized cyclically while others are fixed, avoiding a computationally expensive combinatorial search.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The method's surrogate learning performance was evaluated on two synthetic benchmark functions: Levy (two fidelities, two-dimensional input) and Branin (three fidelities, two-dimensional input), using randomly generated training examples ({130, 65} for Levy; {320, 130, 65} for Branin) and assessed on 100 uniformly sampled test points using normalized Root-Mean-Square-Error (nRMSE) and Mean-Negative-Log-Likelihood (MNLL). For real-world applications, BMBO-DARN was applied to hyperparameter optimization tasks for four machine learning models: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) on CIFAR-10, Online Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) on 20NewsGroups, XGBoost for diabetes diagnosis, and Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN) for solving Burger's equation. Each application involved specific hyperparameters and 2-3 fidelities (e.g., training epochs, number of weak learners, L-BFGS iterations) with corresponding cost ratios. All methods started with 10 random initial queries per fidelity, used a batch size of 5 for batch methods, and ran for 100 queries. Performance was measured by negative log-loss (CNN), perplexity (LDA), log nRMSE (XGBoost, PINN), and accumulated cost (running time). Comparisons included MF-GP-UCB, MF-MES, SHTL, DNN-MFBO, MF-MES-Batch, SF-Batch, SMAC3, Hyperband, BOHB, and a single-query version of BMBO-DARN. Experiments were repeated 5 times, and average results with standard deviations were reported. BMBO-DARN was implemented using PyTorch and Hamiltorch for HMC, with a two-hidden-layer, 40-neuron BNN architecture per fidelity.",
    "Limitations": "The paper primarily highlights the limitations of existing multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization methods, such as their tendency to oversimplify or ignore complex correlations between different fidelities, their inefficiency due to one-by-one querying strategies that cannot utilize parallel computing resources, and the risk of generating highly correlated or redundant queries. It also notes the computational challenge of mutual information calculation and combinatorial search for batch optimization in general BO frameworks, which BMBO-DARN is designed to overcome. However, the paper does not explicitly state specific limitations or weaknesses of BMBO-DARN itself.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper suggests that the proposed method's flexibility allows for switching to other approximate inference approaches, such as stochastic gradient HMC or variational inference, for posterior sampling of Bayesian neural network weights. This implies a potential future research direction in exploring alternative inference mechanisms to possibly improve computational efficiency or scalability beyond the currently adopted Hamiltonian Monte Carlo sampling. No other explicit future research directions for the overall BMBO-DARN framework are mentioned.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null
