    "Experiment Result": "Framework Name: BOIL\nObjective Function Model: Gaussian Process\n  Kernel: Product kernel (combining Radial Basis Function (RBF) for parameter space and RBF for iteration space).\n    Initial `lengthscale_x` (parameter space): 0.02\n    Initial `lengthscale_t` (iteration space): 0.2\n  Noise (`noise_delta`): 5e-4\n  Hyperparameter Optimization: GP hyperparameters and Sigmoid parameters are optimized periodically by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood using multi-start L-BFGS-B gradient descent.\nTraining Time Cost Model: Linear Regression, trained on the concatenation of normalized input hyperparameters (`X`) and normalized number of training iterations (`T`).\nAcquisition Function: Cost-aware Expected Improvement (EI), computed as `log(EI) - log(predicted_computational_cost)`. The incumbent (`y_max` for EI) is set to the maximum of the GP's predictive mean (`mu_max`) over the search space.\nLearning Curve Compression: Sigmoid (Logistic) Preference Function (`apply_one_transform_logistic`)\n  Function: `1.0 / (1 + exp(-growth * (x - midpoint)))`\n  Input `x` scale for Sigmoid: `np.linspace(-6, 6, MaxEpisode)`\n  Parameters (`midpoint`, `growth`): Initialized to `midpoint=0.0`, `growth=1.0`. Optimized jointly with GP hyperparameters by maximizing the GP's log marginal likelihood.\nData Augmentation: Actively selects a subset of intermediate points from observed learning curves.\n  Selection Logic: Points exhibiting maximum GP predictive uncertainty are chosen. This is found by maximizing the `pure_exploration` acquisition function (which is `sqrt(variance)`) on a temporary GP model with fixed input hyperparameters (`x_max`).\n  Conditioning Constraint: The augmentation process is limited. It stops if the natural logarithm of the GP covariance matrix's condition number exceeds a predefined threshold of 15, or if the predictive uncertainty at the selected point falls below `gp.noise_delta + 1e-3`. A maximum of 10 virtual observations per real observation (multiplied by `t_max`) are allowed.\nOptimization of Acquisition Function: Multi-start L-BFGS-B algorithm is used to find the maximum of the cost-aware acquisition function over the search space."

    "Title": "PASHA: Efficient HPO and NAS with Progressive Resource Allocation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Progressive ASHA (PASHA), an extension of ASHA, designed to efficiently tackle hyperparameter optimization (HPO) and neural architecture search (NAS) for machine learning models trained on large datasets with limited computational resources. PASHA dynamically allocates maximum resources for the tuning procedure based on need, automatically stopping early when configuration rankings stabilize. The main contributions include: 1) Introducing PASHA for dynamic resource allocation in HPO/NAS, 2) Empirically demonstrating significant speedups without sacrificing model performance, and 3) Showing its successful combination with sample-efficient strategies like Bayesian Optimization, highlighting its generality.",
    "Methodology": "PASHA extends the ASHA algorithm by incorporating a dynamic resource allocation mechanism inspired by the \"doubling trick\". It starts with a small initial amount of resources and progressively increases them only if the ranking of configurations in the top two rungs has not stabilized. A core component is \"soft ranking,\" which considers configurations equivalent if their performance difference is below a threshold ϵ. This ϵ value is automatically estimated by measuring noise in rankings: it identifies pairs of configurations that repeatedly swap ranks across different resource levels (epochs) and calculates ϵ as the N-th percentile of performance differences for these \"criss-crossing\" pairs. PASHA uses a reduction factor η (similar to ASHA) to prune candidates and includes a user-defined maximum resource R as a safety net, beyond which it defaults to ASHA.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental evaluation aimed to demonstrate faster tuning time and on-par predictive performance compared to ASHA. Experiments consisted of two phases: 1) running the optimizer to evaluate 256 candidate configurations, and 2) retraining the best-identified configuration from scratch using only the training set (for consistency with benchmarks). Benchmarks included NASBench201 for NAS problems (on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet16-120 datasets, with r=1 epoch and R=200 epochs) and the PD1 benchmark for HPO (WMT15 German-English and ImageNet datasets, using xformer and ResNet50 models respectively). HPO tasks optimized four hyperparameters: base learning rate, momentum, polynomial learning rate decay power, and decay steps fraction. All experiments used 4 workers for parallel, asynchronous evaluations. Baselines included ASHA, a \"one-epoch baseline,\" and a \"random baseline,\" with additional 2, 3, and 5-epoch baselines in the appendix. PASHA used N=90th percentile for ϵ estimation and a default reduction factor η=3. Additional experiments explored varying η, combining PASHA with Bayesian Optimization (MOBSTER), and alternative ranking functions (e.g., direct ranking, soft ranking with fixed ϵ, standard deviation-based ϵ, RBO, RRR). The LCBench benchmark was used to analyze performance with limited rungs.",
    "Limitations": "PASHA's benefits, specifically speedup, are limited when the number of available rungs (resource levels) is small. This scenario is observed in benchmarks like LCBench, where the maximum resources (e.g., 50 epochs) and minimum resources (e.g., 1 epoch) offer few opportunities for PASHA to interrupt tuning early. Public benchmarks often fix the minimum resource to one epoch, which can constrain the algorithm's control. The authors recommend having a maximum amount of resources at least 100 times larger than the minimum (with η=3) to maximize PASHA's benefits, suggesting that resources might need to be measured with higher granularity (e.g., gradient updates instead of epochs) in practice.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could investigate how the definition of rungs and resource levels impacts the decision-making process of multi-fidelity algorithms, including PASHA. This would involve understanding the optimal granularities for resource allocation. Another promising direction is to explore combinations of PASHA with transfer-learning techniques specifically designed for multi-fidelity problems, such as RUSH, to achieve further reductions in tuning time.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nfrom syne_tune.optimizer.schedulers.hyperband_promotion import PromotionRungSystem\nimport itertools\n\nclass PASHARungSystem(PromotionRungSystem):\n    \"\"\"\n    Implements PASHA algorithm. It is very similar to ASHA, but it progressively\n    extends the maximum resources if the ranking in the top two current rungs changes.\n\n    A report introducing and evaluating the approach is available at\n    TODO: add link\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        rung_levels,\n        promote_quantiles,\n        metric,\n        mode,\n        resource_attr,\n        max_t,\n        ranking_criterion,\n        epsilon,\n        epsilon_scaling,\n    ):\n        super().__init__(\n            rung_levels, promote_quantiles, metric, mode, resource_attr, max_t\n        )\n        self.ranking_criterion = ranking_criterion\n        # define the index of the current top rung, starting from 1 for the lowest rung\n        #\n        self.current_rung_idx = 2\n        self.rung_levels = rung_levels\n\n        # initialize current maximum resources\n        self.current_max_t = rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]\n\n        self.epsilon = epsilon\n        self.epsilon_scaling = epsilon_scaling\n        if ranking_criterion == 'soft_ranking_auto':\n            self.per_epoch_results = {}\n            self.epoch_to_trials = {}\n            self.current_max_epoch = -1\n\n    # overriding the method in HB promotion to accomodate the increasing max resources level\n    def _effective_max_t(self):\n        return self.current_max_t\n\n    def _get_top_rungs_rankings(self, num_rungs=2):\n        \"\"\"\n        Look at the current top two rungs and get the rankings of the configurations.\n        The rungs can be empty, in which case we will return a list with 0 or 1 elements.\n        Normally the list will include rankings for both rungs.\n\n        The rankings are stored as a list of tuples (trial_id, rank, value).\n\n        Lower values have lower ranks, starting from zero. For example:\n        [('0', 0, 10.0), ('1', 3, 19.6), ('2', 2, 14.3), ('3', 1, 11.6)]\n\n        :param num_rungs: int describing how many top rungs to return\n        :return: rankings\n            List of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)\n        \"\"\"\n        rankings = []\n        # be careful, self._rungs is ordered with the highest resources level in the beginning\n        rungs = [self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx + e] for e in range(num_rungs)]\n        for rung in rungs:\n            if rung.data != {}:\n                trial_ids = rung.data.keys()\n                values = []\n                for trial_id in trial_ids:\n                    values.append(rung.data[trial_id][0])\n                # order specifies where the value should be placed in the sorted list\n                values_order = np.array(values).argsort()\n                # calling argsort on the order will give us the ranking\n                values_ranking = values_order.argsort()\n                ranking = list(zip(trial_ids, values_ranking, values))\n\n                rankings.append(ranking)\n\n        return rankings\n\n    def _get_sorted_top_rungs(self, rankings):\n        \"\"\"\n        Sort the configurations in the top rung and the previous rung.\n        Filter out the configurations from the previous rung that\n        are not in the top rung.\n\n        :param rankings: list of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)\n        return: sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung\n        \"\"\"\n        # filter only the relevant configurations from the earlier rung\n        top_rung_keys = set([e[0] for e in rankings[0]])\n        corresponding_previous_rung_trials = filter(\n            lambda e: e[0] in top_rung_keys, rankings[1]\n        )\n        # if we try to maximize the objective, we need to reverse the ranking\n        if self._mode == \"max\":\n            reverse = True\n        else:\n            reverse = False\n\n        sorted_top_rung = sorted(rankings[0], key=lambda e: e[1], reverse=reverse)\n        sorted_previous_rung = sorted(\n            corresponding_previous_rung_trials, key=lambda e: e[1], reverse=reverse\n        )\n        return sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung\n\n    def _evaluate_soft_ranking(self, sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung) -> bool:\n        \"\"\"\n        Soft ranking creates groups of similarly performing configurations\n        and increases the resources only if a configuration goes outside of\n        its group.\n\n        :param sorted_top_rung: list of tuple(trial_id, rank, score)\n        :param sorted_previous_rung: list of tuple(sorted_top_rung, rank, score)\n        :return: keep_current_budget\n        \"\"\"\n        keep_current_budget = True\n        if len(sorted_previous_rung) < 2:\n            epsilon = 0.0\n        elif self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_std\":\n            epsilon = (\n                np.std([e[2] for e in sorted_previous_rung]) * self.epsilon_scaling\n            )\n        elif (\n            self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_median_dst\"\n            or self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_mean_dst\"\n        ):\n            scores = [e[2] for e in sorted_previous_rung]\n            distances = [\n                abs(e1 - e2)\n                for idx1, e1 in enumerate(scores)\n                for idx2, e2 in enumerate(scores)\n                if idx1 != idx2\n            ]\n            if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_mean_dst\":\n                epsilon = np.mean(distances) * self.epsilon_scaling\n            elif self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_median_dst\":\n                epsilon = np.median(distances) * self.epsilon_scaling\n            else:\n                raise ValueError(\n                    \"Ranking criterion {} is not supported\".format(\n                        self.ranking_criterion\n                    )\n                )\n        else:\n            epsilon = self.epsilon\n\n        # create groups of configurations with similar performance\n        previous_rung_groups = []\n        for idx, item in enumerate(sorted_previous_rung):\n            current_rung_group = [item[0]]\n            # add configurations that are after the current configuration\n            for idx_after in range(idx + 1, len(sorted_previous_rung)):\n                new_item = sorted_previous_rung[idx_after]\n\n                if self._mode == \"max\":\n                    if new_item[2] < item[2] - epsilon:\n                        break\n                else:\n                    if new_item[2] > item[2] + epsilon:\n                        break\n                current_rung_group.append(new_item[0])\n            # add configurations that are before the current configuration\n            for idx_before in range(idx - 1, -1, -1):\n                new_item = sorted_previous_rung[idx_before]\n                if self._mode == \"max\":\n                    if new_item[2] > item[2] + epsilon:\n                        break\n                else:\n                    if new_item[2] < item[2] - epsilon:\n                        break\n                current_rung_group.append(new_item[0])\n            previous_rung_groups.append(set(current_rung_group))\n\n        # evaluate if a configuration has switched its group\n        for idx, item in enumerate(sorted_top_rung):\n            if item[0] not in previous_rung_groups[idx]:\n                keep_current_budget = False\n                break\n\n        return keep_current_budget\n\n    def _update_epsilon(self):\n        \"\"\"\n        This function is used to automatically calculate the value of epsilon.\n        It finds the configurations which swapped their rankings across rungs\n        and estimates the value of epsilon as the 90th percentile of the difference\n        between their performance in the previous rung.\n\n        The original value of epsilon is kept if no suitable configurations were found.\n        \"\"\"\n\n        seen_pairs = set()\n        noisy_cfg_distances = []\n        top_epoch = min(self.current_max_epoch, self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx].level)\n        bottom_epoch = min(self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx+1].level, self.current_max_epoch)\n        for epoch in range(top_epoch, bottom_epoch, -1):\n            if len(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch]) > 1:\n                for pair in itertools.combinations(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch], 2):\n                    c1, c2 = pair[0], pair[1]\n                    if (c1, c2) not in seen_pairs:\n                        seen_pairs.add((c1, c2))\n                        p1, p2 = self.per_epoch_results[c1][epoch], self.per_epoch_results[c2][epoch]\n                        cond = p1 > p2\n\n                        opposite_order = False\n                        same_order_after_opposite = False\n                        # now we need to check the earlier epochs to see if at any point they had a different order\n                        for prev_epoch in range(epoch - 1, 0, -1):\n                            pp1, pp2 = self.per_epoch_results[c1][prev_epoch], self.per_epoch_results[c2][prev_epoch]\n                            p_cond = pp1 > pp2\n                            if p_cond == (not cond):\n                                opposite_order = True\n                            if opposite_order and p_cond == cond:\n                                same_order_after_opposite = True\n                                break\n\n                        if opposite_order and same_order_after_opposite:\n                            noisy_cfg_distances.append(abs(p1 - p2))\n\n        if len(noisy_cfg_distances) > 0:\n            self.epsilon = np.percentile(noisy_cfg_distances, 90)\n            if str(self.epsilon) == 'nan':\n                raise ValueError('Epsilon became nan') \n\n    def _update_per_epoch_results(self, trial_id, result):\n        if trial_id not in self.per_epoch_results:\n            self.per_epoch_results[trial_id] = {}\n        self.per_epoch_results[trial_id][result[self._resource_attr]] = result[self._metric]\n\n        if result[self._resource_attr] not in self.epoch_to_trials:\n            self.epoch_to_trials[result[self._resource_attr]] = set() \n        self.epoch_to_trials[result[self._resource_attr]].add(trial_id)\n\n        if result[self._resource_attr] > self.current_max_epoch:\n            self.current_max_epoch = result[self._resource_attr]\n\n    def _decide_resource_increase(self, rankings) -> bool:\n        \"\"\"\n        Decide if to increase the resources given the current rankings.\n        Currently we look at the rankings and if elements in the first list\n        have the same order also in the second list, we keep the current resource\n        budget. If the rankings are different, we will increase the budget.\n\n        The rankings can only be incorrect if we have rankings for both rungs.\n\n        :param rankings: list of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)\n        :return: not keep_current_budget\n        \"\"\"\n        if len(rankings) == 2:\n            sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung = self._get_sorted_top_rungs(rankings)\n        else:\n            return False\n\n        keep_current_budget = self._evaluate_soft_ranking(\n            sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung\n        )\n\n        return not keep_current_budget\n\n    def on_task_report(self, trial_id: str, result: dict, skip_rungs: int) -> dict:\n        \"\"\"\n        Apart from calling the superclass method, we also check the rankings\n        and decides if to increase the current maximum resources.\n        \"\"\"\n        ret_dict = super().on_task_report(trial_id, result, skip_rungs)\n\n        if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_auto\":\n            self._update_per_epoch_results(trial_id, result)\n            self._update_epsilon()\n\n        # check the rankings and decide if to increase the current maximum resources\n        rankings = self._get_top_rungs_rankings(num_rungs=2)\n        increase_resources = self._decide_resource_increase(rankings)\n\n        # we have a maximum amount of resources that PASHA can use\n        # the resources should not increase indefinitely\n        if increase_resources:\n            if self.current_rung_idx < len(self._rungs):\n                self.current_rung_idx += 1\n                # be careful, self.rung_levels is ordered with the highest resources level at the end\n                # moreover, since we use rung levels for counting both from the beginning and from the end of the list\n                # we need to remember that counting from the beginning it's zero indexed\n                self.current_max_t = self.rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]\n            else:\n                self.current_max_t = self.max_t\n\n        return ret_dict\n",
    "Experiment Result": "PASHA extends the ASHA algorithm by dynamically adjusting resource allocation. It starts with a small initial amount of resources and progressively increases them (`self.current_rung_idx` and `self.current_max_t` in `PASHARungSystem`) if the ranking of configurations in the top two rungs has not stabilized. A core component is \"soft ranking,\" which considers configurations equivalent if their performance difference is below a threshold ϵ.\n\nThe ϵ value is automatically estimated (`_update_epsilon` method in `PASHARungSystem`) by measuring noise in rankings: it identifies pairs of configurations that repeatedly swap ranks across different resource levels (epochs) and calculates ϵ as the 90th percentile of performance differences for these \"criss-crossing\" pairs. PASHA uses a reduction factor η (similar to ASHA) to prune candidates and includes a user-defined maximum resource R as a safety net, beyond which it defaults to ASHA.\n\nExperimental settings for PASHA are passed via `rung_system_kwargs` to the `HyperbandScheduler` and include:\n- `ranking_criterion`: Can be 'soft_ranking', 'soft_ranking_std', 'soft_ranking_median_dst', or 'soft_ranking_mean_dst'. When set to 'soft_ranking_auto', it triggers automatic epsilon estimation.\n- `epsilon`: A float value for the performance difference threshold. Used with 'soft_ranking'.\n- `epsilon_scaling`: A float multiplier for the automatically estimated epsilon.\n- Initial `current_rung_idx`: Set to 2, meaning it starts considering the top two rungs.\n\nExample instantiation in `benchmarking/cli/scheduler_factory.py`:\n```python\n        if scheduler == \"hyperband_pasha\":\n            rung_system_kwargs = scheduler_options.get(\"rung_system_kwargs\", dict())\n            for name, tp in (\n                (\"ranking_criterion\", str),\n                (\"epsilon\", float),\n                (\"epsilon_scaling\", float),\n            ):\n                name_cl = \"pasha_\" + name\n                v = params.get(name_cl)\n                if v is not None:\n                    rung_system_kwargs[name] = tp(v)\n            if rung_system_kwargs:\n                scheduler_options[\"rung_system_kwargs\"] = rung_system_kwargs\n```"

    "Title": "Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper presents Bayesian Optimization for Iterative Learning (BOIL), an approach that exploits the iterative nature of learning algorithms for efficient hyperparameter tuning. It introduces an algorithm to optimize the learning curve of a machine learning model using training curve compression, rather than just final performance. This includes an approach to learn the compression curve from the data and a data augmentation technique for increased sample-efficiency. The method balances the benefit of assessing hyperparameter settings over additional training steps against their computational cost. It demonstrates efficiency in tuning hyperparameters for deep reinforcement learning (DRL) agents and convolutional neural networks (CNNs), outperforming existing baselines in identifying optimal hyperparameters in minimal time.",
    "Methodology": "The BOIL framework models the cost-sensitive black-box function f(x,t) using a Gaussian Process (GP) with a product kernel over hyperparameters (x) and training iterations (t). The cost function c(x,t) is approximated by a linear regressor. The acquisition function selects the next hyperparameter-iteration pair by maximizing a modified Expected Improvement criterion divided by the predicted cost, balancing exploitation, exploration, and cost efficiency. A key component is training curve compression, where the entire learning curve r(·|x,t) is transformed into a single numeric score using a Sigmoid (Logistic) preference function l(·|m0,g0). The parameters (m0, g0) of this function are learned directly from the data by maximizing the GP log marginal likelihood, favoring stable, high-performing configurations. To avoid ill-conditioning, data augmentation is performed by selectively adding points from the observed curve at locations of maximum GP predictive uncertainty, with the number of augmented points adaptively chosen to maintain a well-conditioned covariance matrix.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The algorithm's efficiency was demonstrated on two DRL agents and a CNN across five environments/datasets. DRL tasks included tuning Dueling DQN on CartPole-v0 and Advantage Actor Critic (A2C) on InvertedPendulum-v2 and Reacher-v2 (using OpenAI Gym and Mujoco environments). CNN tuning was performed on the SVHN and CIFAR10 datasets. Experiments were averaged over 20 independent runs on an NVIDIA 1080 GTX GPU using TensorFlow. Square-exponential kernels were used for the GP, with parameters estimated by maximizing marginal likelihood. A maximum of 15 augmented points were used, with a threshold of 20 for the natural log of the GP condition number. Baselines included Hyperband and Continuous Multi-task/Fidelity BO (CM-T/F-BO), which extends discrete multi-task BO to continuous settings.",
    "Limitations": "Existing iteration-efficient BO algorithms, like Freeze-thaw BO, rely on assumptions such as exponential decay of training loss, which may not be applicable in DRL due to its unpredictable and noisy reward curves. Naively augmenting the GP model with a full curve of intermediate training points can lead to redundant information and serious ill-conditioning issues for the GP covariance matrix, especially in noisy DRL settings. Hyperband, while effective, can be misled in noisy DRL tasks by large fluctuations in early performance, making it difficult to allocate evaluation effort and revise choices without restarting the sampling process. A broader impact limitation notes that increased automation in ML pipelines, like with BOIL, could distance humans from the modeling process, potentially making it harder to identify critical failures and contributing to the growing opacity of ML models.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that the proposed BOIL approach is not limited to machine learning algorithms but can be generally applied to any process exhibiting an iterative structure that can be exploited. One specific example given is the optimization of manufacturing pipelines, where factory settings could be adjusted to increase productivity. More broadly, in the context of increasing automation, future work should encourage practitioners to critically reflect on potential biases embedded in datasets and models used in automated processes. Furthermore, it is important to leverage the growing body of work on interpretability of machine learning models to rigorously analyze the outcomes of automated training procedures, ensuring beneficial and transparent real-world policy making.",
    "Experiment Code": "import numpy as np\nfrom bayes_opt.acquisition_functions import AcquisitionFunction, unique_rows\nfrom bayes_opt import GaussianProcess\nfrom bayes_opt import       scaler.fit(self.SearchSpace[:-1,:].T)\n        \n        scalerT = MinMaxScaler()\n        SearchSpace_T=np.atleast_2d(self.SearchSpace[-1,:]).T\n        scalerT.fit(SearchSpace_T)\n\n     "Experiment Result": "The BOIL framework employs a Gaussian Process (GP) with a product RBF kernel, k({x,t},{x',t'}) = k(x,x') * k(t,t'), to model the cost-sensitive black-box function f(x,t). The kernel hyperparameters, specifically lengthscale_x (for hyperparameters x) and lengthscale_t (for training iterations t), along with noise_delta (noise variance), are initialized to lengthscale_x=0.02, lengthscale_t=0.2, and noise_delta=5e-4.\n\nTraining curve compression is achieved using a Sigmoid (Logistic) preference function, l(·|m0,g0), to transform learning curves into single numeric scores. The parameters m0 (midpoint) and g0 (growth) of this function, initially 0.0 and 1.0 respectively, are learned simultaneously with the GP lengthscales by maximizing the GP log marginal likelihood. This optimization process utilizes multi-start L-BFGS-B (from scipy.optimize.minimize) and is performed every 2*self.dim iterations.\n\nThe cost function c(x,t) is approximated by a linear regressor, specifically sklearn.linear_model.LinearRegression. The acquisition function is a modified Expected Improvement (EI) criterion, defined as log(utility) - log(mean_cost) (or log(1+exp(utility))/log(1+exp(mean_cost))), with the acq_name set to \"ei_mu_max\", indicating that the maximum of the GP mean is used as the incumbent for EI calculation. This acquisition function is maximized using multi-start L-BFGS-B optimization.\n\nTo address ill-conditioning, data augmentation through 'virtual observations' is implemented. These virtual points are added along the training curve for a given hyperparameter configuration. The locations for these virtual observations are strategically chosen by maximizing GP predictive uncertainty within the observed training iteration range. The number of augmented points is adaptively limited by max_n_augmentation (default 10) times the current maximum observed training iteration. Augmentation ceases if the log of the GP covariance matrix condition number exceeds threshold_cond (default 15) or if the predictive uncertainty falls below a threshold (noise_delta + 1e-3). Initial experimental setup includes n_init_points (default 3) randomly sampled points, for which virtual observations are also generated.\n\nFor internal processing, utility scores (Y) are standardized to a N(0,1) distribution for GP fitting, while cost values (Y_cost) are normalized to the [0,1] range."

    "Title": "Scaling Laws for Hyperparameter Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces Deep Power Laws (DPL), a novel multi-fidelity Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) method that leverages the power-law nature of learning curves to efficiently tune hyperparameters for Deep Learning (DL) models. DPL employs an ensemble of neural networks to predict future performance from partial learning curves, dynamically deciding which configurations to incrementally train. The key finding is that DPL achieves state-of-the-art results across diverse benchmarks and significantly outperforms existing HPO baselines, making HPO for DL more feasible.",
    "Methodology": "DPL proposes a probabilistic surrogate for Bayesian optimization (BO) based on an ensemble of K diverse deep power law functions. Each ensemble member is a 2-layer feedforward neural network with 128 units per layer, conditioned to map hyperparameter configurations (lambda) to power-law coefficients (alpha, beta, gamma) for predicting validation loss ˆf(lambda, b) = g(lambda)alpha + g(lambda)beta * b^-g(lambda)gamma. The ensemble provides posterior mean and variance for predictions. Expected Improvement (EI) is used as the acquisition function to select the next promising hyperparameter configuration. A multi-fidelity strategy then advances the selected configuration by a small budget (bstep, e.g., one epoch) rather than training until full convergence, allowing for dynamic pausing and incremental training of configurations.",
    "Experimental Setup": "DPL was compared against 7 state-of-the-art HPO baselines (Random Search, Hyperband, ASHA, BOHB, DEHB, SMAC, BOCA) on 3 main benchmarks covering 59 diverse tasks: LCBench (tabular data, 2,000 feedforward NN configurations, 35 datasets), PD1 (deep learning architectures like Transformers on large vision, statistical modeling, and protein sequence datasets, varying LC lengths from 5 to 1414, 807 to 2807 configurations), and TaskSet (RNN text classification tasks across 5 search spaces, focusing on Adam8p with 1000 configurations and 8 continuous hyperparameters). Additionally, a custom nanoGPT-Bench (GPT-2 model on OpenWebText dataset for LLM HPO, ablating embedding sizes from 0.3M to 30M parameters) and a CIFAR10 task with EfficientNetV2 were used for specific analyses. Evaluation metrics included balanced accuracy (LCBench), log-likelihood loss (TaskSet), and accuracy (PD1, CIFAR10), measured as regret against an oracle. Experiments were run on a CPU cluster with Intel Xeon E5-2630v4 CPUs (2 cores, 12GB memory per experiment). Time comparisons used normalized wall clock time.",
    "Limitations": "The uncertainty estimation derived from the Deep Ensemble approach was found to be suboptimal compared to standard BO surrogates like Gaussian Processes. Additionally, training an ensemble of models introduces additional computational costs due to the necessity of training multiple power law models.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work includes investigating the combination of power laws with Gaussian Processes to potentially improve uncertainty estimation. Another direction is to explore additional fidelity types beyond training epochs and model size.",
    "Experiment Code": "class ConditionedPowerLaw(nn.Module):    def __init__(        self,        nr_initial_features=10,        nr_units=200,        nr_layers=3,        use_learning_curve: bool = True,        kernel_size: int = 3,        nr_filters: int = 4,        nr_cnn_layers: int = 2,    ):        super(ConditionedPowerLaw, self).__init__()        self.use_learning_curve = use_learning_curve        self.kernel_size = kernel_size        self.nr_filters = nr_filters        self.nr_cnn_layers = nr_cnn_layers        self.act_func = torch.nn.LeakyReLU()        self.last_act_func = torch.nn.GLU()        self.tan_func = torch.nn.Tanh()        self.batch_norm = torch.nn.BatchNorm1d        layers = []        nr_initial_features = nr_initial_features        if self.use_learning_curve:            nr_initial_features = nr_initial_features + nr_filters        layers.append(nn.Linear(nr_initial_features, nr_units))        layers.append(self.act_func)        for i in range(2, nr_layers + 1):            layers.append(nn.Linear(nr_units, nr_units))            layers.append(self.act_func)        last_layer = nn.Linear(nr_units, 3)        layers.append(last_layer)        self.layers = torch.nn.Sequential(*layers)        cnn_part = []        if use_learning_curve:            cnn_part.append(                nn.Conv1d(                    in_channels=2,                    kernel_size=(self.kernel_size,),                    out_channels=self.nr_filters,                ),            )            for i in range(1, self.nr_cnn_layers):                cnn_part.append(self.act_func)                cnn_part.append(                    nn.Conv1d(                        in_channels=self.nr_filters,                        kernel_size=(self.kernel_size,),                        out_channels=self.nr_filters,                    ),                ),            cnn_part.append(nn.AdaptiveAvgPool1d(1))        self.cnn = nn.Sequential(*cnn_part)    def forward(        self,        x: torch.Tensor,        predict_budgets: torch.Tensor,        evaluated_budgets: torch.Tensor,        learning_curves: torch.Tensor,    ):        if self.use_learning_curve:            lc_features = self.cnn(learning_curves)            lc_features = torch.squeeze(lc_features, 2)            x = torch.cat((x, lc_features), dim=1)        x = self.layers(x)        alphas = x[:, 0]        betas = x[:, 1]        gammas = x[:, 2]        output = torch.add(            alphas,            torch.mul(                self.last_act_func(torch.cat((betas, betas))),                torch.pow(                    predict_budgets,                    torch.mul(self.last_act_func(torch.cat((gammas, gammas))), -1)                )            ),        )        return output",
    "Experiment Result": "The Deep Power Law (DPL) method utilizes a probabilistic surrogate for Bayesian optimization. Each ensemble member consists of a 2-layer feedforward neural network with 128 units per layer, conditioned to map hyperparameter configurations to power-law coefficients (alpha, beta, gamma). The power-law function used is `output = alpha + beta * budget^-gamma`. The ensemble size (K) is 5. The surrogate models are trained for 250 epochs initially (`nr_epochs`), and refined for 20 epochs (`refine_nr_epochs`). The learning rate for the surrogate is 0.001, and the batch size is 64 for training and `refine_batch_size` 64 for refining. The acquisition function used for selecting the next promising hyperparameter configuration is Expected Improvement (EI). A multi-fidelity strategy is employed where the selected configuration is advanced by a `fantasize_step` of 1 epoch, rather than training until full convergence. The overall optimization objective is minimization of the validation loss. The initial configurations are 1 random configuration evaluated for 1 epoch. The model can handle learning curves with missing values by filling them with either 'zero' or 'last' observed value (defaulting to 'zero')."

    "Title": "Hyperparameter Optimization through Neural Network Partitioning",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes a novel and efficient method for hyperparameter optimization in neural networks, inspired by marginal likelihood, that requires no validation data and only a single training run. The key innovation is partitioning the training data into K data shards and the neural network model into K parameter partitions. Each partition is optimized only on specific data shards, and combining these into subnetworks allows defining an \"out-of-training-sample\" loss for hyperparameter optimization. This approach is demonstrated to be computationally cheaper than alternative marginal likelihood methods and applicable to various hyperparameters, including in challenging settings like federated learning.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves 'Partitioned Neural Networks'. The training dataset D is divided into C chunks (D1,...,DC), and the neural network parameters w are partitioned into C corresponding partitions (w1,...,wC). Subnetworks w(k)s are constructed such that w(k)s = concat(w1,...,wk, ˆwk+1,..., ˆwC), where ˆwi are fixed default values. Each partition wk is optimized on data from D1:k using its respective subnetwork w(k)s. Hyperparameters ψ are optimized using an objective function LML which approximates a lower-bound on the marginal likelihood, calculated as the sum of 'out-of-training-sample' losses, i.e., log p(Dk|w(k-1)s,ψ) for k=2 to C. Both parameter and hyperparameter updates are performed using stochastic gradient descent. Random weight partitioning is primarily used, assigning a fixed proportion of weights in each layer to a partition, with future chunk weights initialized to default values.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on several datasets and tasks: a toy input selection task, learning affine data augmentations on MNIST, CIFAR10, TinyImagenet, and their rotated variants (rotMNIST, rotCIFAR10, rotTinyImagenet). Architectures included fully-connected MLPs, fixupResNets (8 and 14 layers), ResNet-50 with GroupNorm(2), and a CNN for MNIST. Baselines included standard training, Augerino, Differentiable Laplace, last-layer marginal likelihood, and FedAvg for federated learning. Validation methods involved tracking the LML objective, test accuracy, and federated validation accuracy. Data subsets were used to evaluate performance in low-data regimes. The federated learning setup involved non-i.i.d. data partitioning across clients with label-skew and rotation-skew, optimizing data augmentation parameters and dropout rates.",
    "Limitations": "The method requires an additional forward-backward pass for hyperparameter updates, incurring computational costs, though less than existing marginal likelihood approximations. Partitioned networks generally require more training iterations to converge. Partitioning the network inherently constrains its capacity, potentially causing some loss in final model performance compared to a fully trained, non-partitioned network with optimally tuned hyperparameters. The method also introduces an additional hyperparameter: the partitioning strategy (number of chunks, relative proportions of data per chunk, and parameter proportions per partition), which might require tuning for optimal performance.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include investigating alternative partitioning schemes (e.g., dynamic partitioning of network parameters during training) and exploring different sequential updating schemes for partitions in federated learning. Also, opportunities for alleviating performance loss due to network partitioning, such as adjusting training rounds or increasing initial network capacity, are left for future work. Additionally, exploring different strategies for reducing computational overhead (e.g., interleaving hyperparameter updates less frequently) and gradient accumulation schemes for lower variance estimates are suggested.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "PASHA: Efficient HPO and NAS with Progressive Resource Allocation",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces PASHA (Progressive Asynchronous Successive Halving), an extension of ASHA, designed to make Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) and Neural Architecture Search (NAS) more efficient and less computationally expensive, particularly for models trained on large datasets. PASHA dynamically allocates maximum resources for the tuning procedure, stopping early when the ranking of configurations stabilizes, thereby consuming significantly fewer computational resources than ASHA while identifying similarly well-performing configurations and architectures. The approach is also shown to be combinable with sample-efficient strategies like Bayesian Optimization, highlighting its generality and ability to reduce the entry barrier for HPO and NAS.",
    "Methodology": "PASHA extends ASHA by incorporating a progressive resource allocation strategy, inspired by the 'doubling trick'. It begins with a small initial amount of resources and gradually increases them only if the ranking of configurations within the top two rungs (rounds of promotion) remains inconsistent. The key to its efficiency is the ability to automatically stop early when configuration rankings stabilize. PASHA utilizes a 'soft ranking' mechanism, where configurations are considered equivalent if their performance difference is below a threshold epsilon (ϵ). This ϵ value is automatically estimated by measuring noise in rankings, specifically by identifying 'criss-crossing' behaviors between pairs of configurations across different resource levels (epochs), indicating similarly performing configurations. A user-defined minimum resource 'r' is easier to set than ASHA's fixed maximum 'R', and a global maximum 'R' acts as a safety net to prevent indefinite resource increases.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The evaluation involved two main phases: 1) running the hyperparameter optimizer until 256 candidate configurations were evaluated, and 2) retraining the best identified configuration from scratch (using only the training set for experiments, but acknowledging real-world use of train+validation). For NAS experiments, NASBench201 (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, ImageNet16-120) was used. For HPO, two large-scale tasks from the PD1 benchmark (WMT15 German-English with xformer and ImageNet with ResNet50) were employed. Additional experiments were conducted on LCBench. Performance was measured by the best accuracy after retraining and runtime (HPO time only). Baselines included standard ASHA, a 'one-epoch baseline' (training all configurations for one epoch), and a 'random baseline'. PASHA was also integrated with Bayesian Optimization searchers (MOBSTER) for comparison. Experiments used 4 parallel workers, a 90th percentile for ϵ estimation, and reduction factors (η) of 3 (default), 2, and 4. Results were averaged over multiple random seeds.",
    "Limitations": "PASHA's benefits are modest when the number of available rungs is small, as there are fewer opportunities to interrupt the tuning process early. This was demonstrated on the LCBench benchmark. Public benchmarks often fix minimum and maximum resource levels (e.g., 1 to 50 epochs), limiting the flexibility for algorithms like PASHA. For optimal speedups, it is recommended to have a maximum amount of resources at least 100 times larger than the minimum when using a reduction factor of 3. This may necessitate defining resources with higher granularity, such as in terms of gradient updates rather than epochs.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could investigate how the definition of rungs and resource levels impacts the decision-making of multi-fidelity algorithms, including PASHA, to maximize speedup opportunities. Another promising direction is to test combinations of PASHA with transfer-learning techniques specifically designed for multi-fidelity problems, such as RUSH, to achieve further reductions in tuning time.",
    "Experiment Code": "if scheduler == \"hyperband_pasha\":    rung_system_kwargs = scheduler_options.get(\"rung_system_kwargs\", dict())    for name, tp in (        (\"ranking_criterion\", str),        (\"epsilon\", float),        (\"epsilon_scaling\", float),    ):        name_cl = \"pasha_\" + name        v = params.get(name_cl)        if v is not None:            rung_system_kwargs[name] = tp(v)    if rung_system_kwargs:        scheduler_options[\"rung_system_kwargs\"] = rung_system_kwargsFile Path: syne_tune/optimizer/schedulers/hyperband_pasha.pyContent:import numpy as npfrom syne_tune.optimizer.schedulers.hyperband_promotion import PromotionRungSystemimport itertoolsclass PASHARungSystem(PromotionRungSystem):    \"\"\"    Implements PASHA algorithm. It is very similar to ASHA, but it progressively    extends the maximum resources if the ranking in the top two current rungs changes.    A report introducing and evaluating the approach is available at    TODO: add link    \"\"\"    def __init__(        self,        rung_levels,        promote_quantiles,        metric,        mode,        resource_attr,        max_t,        ranking_criterion,        epsilon,        epsilon_scaling,    ):        super().__init__(            rung_levels, promote_quantiles, metric, mode, resource_attr, max_t        )        self.ranking_criterion = ranking_criterion        # define the index of the current top rung, starting from 1 for the lowest rung        #        self.current_rung_idx = 2        self.rung_levels = rung_levels        # initialize current maximum resources        self.current_max_t = rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]        self.epsilon = epsilon        self.epsilon_scaling = epsilon_scaling        if ranking_criterion == 'soft_ranking_auto':            self.per_epoch_results = {}            self.epoch_to_trials = {}            self.current_max_epoch = -1    # overriding the method in HB promotion to accomodate the increasing max resources level    def _effective_max_t(self):        return self.current_max_t    def _get_top_rungs_rankings(self, num_rungs=2):        \"\"\"        Look at the current top two rungs and get the rankings of the configurations.        The rungs can be empty, in which case we will return a list with 0 or 1 elements.        Normally the list will include rankings for both rungs.        The rankings are stored as a list of tuples (trial_id, rank, value).        Lower values have lower ranks, starting from zero. For example:        [('0', 0, 10.0), ('1', 3, 19.6), ('2', 2, 14.3), ('3', 1, 11.6)]        :param num_rungs: int describing how many top rungs to return        :return: rankings            List of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        \"\"\"        rankings = []        # be careful, self._rungs is ordered with the highest resources level in the beginning        rungs = [self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx + e] for e in range(num_rungs)]        for rung in rungs:            if rung.data != {}:                trial_ids = rung.data.keys()                values = []                for trial_id in trial_ids:                    values.append(rung.data[trial_id][0])                # order specifies where the value should be placed in the sorted list                values_order = np.array(values).argsort()                # calling argsort on the order will give us the ranking                values_ranking = values_order.argsort()                ranking = list(zip(trial_ids, values_ranking, values))                rankings.append(ranking)        return rankings    def _get_sorted_top_rungs(self, rankings):        \"\"\"        Sort the configurations in the top rung and the previous rung.        Filter out the configurations from the previous rung that        are not in the top rung.        :param rankings: list of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        return: sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung        \"\"\"        # filter only the relevant configurations from the earlier rung        top_rung_keys = set([e[0] for e in rankings[0]])        corresponding_previous_rung_trials = filter(            lambda e: e[0] in top_rung_keys, rankings[1]        )        # if we try to maximize the objective, we need to reverse the ranking        if self._mode == \"max\":            reverse = True        else:            reverse = False        sorted_top_rung = sorted(rankings[0], key=lambda e: e[1], reverse=reverse)        sorted_previous_rung = sorted(            corresponding_previous_rung_trials, key=lambda e: e[1], reverse=reverse        )        return sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung    def _evaluate_soft_ranking(self, sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung) -> bool:        \"\"\"        Soft ranking creates groups of similarly performing configurations        and increases the resources only if a configuration goes outside of        its group.        :param sorted_top_rung: list of tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        :param sorted_previous_rung: list of tuple(sorted_top_rung, rank, score)        :return: keep_current_budget        \"\"\"        keep_current_budget = True        if len(sorted_previous_rung) < 2:            epsilon = 0.0        elif self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_std\":            epsilon = (                np.std([e[2] for e in sorted_previous_rung]) * self.epsilon_scaling            )        elif (            self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_median_dst\"            or self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_mean_dst\"        ):            scores = [e[2] for e in sorted_previous_rung]            distances = [                abs(e1 - e2)                for idx1, e1 in enumerate(scores)                for idx2, e2 in enumerate(scores)                if idx1 != idx2            ]            if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_mean_dst\":                epsilon = np.mean(distances) * self.epsilon_scaling            elif self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_median_dst\":                epsilon = np.median(distances) * self.epsilon_scaling            else:                raise ValueError(                    \"Ranking criterion {} is not supported\".format(                        self.ranking_criterion                    )                )        else:            epsilon = self.epsilon        # create groups of configurations with similar performance        previous_rung_groups = []        for idx, item in enumerate(sorted_previous_rung):            current_rung_group = [item[0]]            # add configurations that are after the current configuration            for idx_after in range(idx + 1, len(sorted_previous_rung)):                new_item = sorted_previous_rung[idx_after]                if self._mode == \"max\":                    if new_item[2] < item[2] - epsilon:                        break                else:                    if new_item[2] > item[2] + epsilon:                        break                current_rung_group.append(new_item[0])            # add configurations that are before the current configuration            for idx_before in range(idx - 1, -1, -1):                new_item = sorted_previous_rung[idx_before]                if self._mode == \"max\":                    if new_item[2] > item[2] + epsilon:                        break                else:                    if new_item[2] < item[2] - epsilon:                        break                current_rung_group.append(new_item[0])            previous_rung_groups.append(set(current_rung_group))        # evaluate if a configuration has switched its group        for idx, item in enumerate(sorted_top_rung):            if item[0] not in previous_rung_groups[idx]:                keep_current_budget = False                break        return keep_current_budget    def _update_epsilon(self):        \"\"\"        This function is used to automatically calculate the value of epsilon.        It finds the configurations which swapped their rankings across rungs        and estimates the value of epsilon as the 90th percentile of the difference        between their performance in the previous rung.        The original value of epsilon is kept if no suitable configurations were found.        \"\"\"        seen_pairs = set()        noisy_cfg_distances = []        top_epoch = min(self.current_max_epoch, self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx].level)        bottom_epoch = min(self._rungs[-self.current_rung_idx+1].level, self.current_max_epoch)        for epoch in range(top_epoch, bottom_epoch, -1):            if len(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch]) > 1:                for pair in itertools.combinations(self.epoch_to_trials[epoch], 2):                    c1, c2 = pair[0], pair[1]                    if (c1, c2) not in seen_pairs:                        seen_pairs.add((c1, c2))                        p1, p2 = self.per_epoch_results[c1][epoch], self.per_epoch_results[c2][epoch]                        cond = p1 > p2                        opposite_order = False                        same_order_after_opposite = False                        # now we need to check the earlier epochs to see if at any point they had a different order                        for prev_epoch in range(epoch - 1, 0, -1):                            pp1, pp2 = self.per_epoch_results[c1][prev_epoch], self.per_epoch_results[c2][prev_epoch]                            p_cond = pp1 > pp2                            if p_cond == (not cond):                                opposite_order = True                            if opposite_order and p_cond == cond:                                same_order_after_opposite = True                                break                        if opposite_order and same_order_after_opposite:                            noisy_cfg_distances.append(abs(p1 - p2))        if len(noisy_cfg_distances) > 0:            self.epsilon = np.percentile(noisy_cfg_distances, 90)            if str(self.epsilon) == 'nan':                raise ValueError('Epsilon became nan')     def _update_per_epoch_results(self, trial_id, result):        if trial_id not in self.per_epoch_results:            self.per_epoch_results[trial_id] = {}        self.per_epoch_results[trial_id][result[self._resource_attr]] = result[self._metric]        if result[self._resource_attr] not in self.epoch_to_trials:            self.epoch_to_trials[result[self._resource_attr]] = set()         self.epoch_to_trials[result[self._resource_attr]].add(trial_id)        if result[self._resource_attr] > self.current_max_epoch:            self.current_max_epoch = result[self._resource_attr]    def _decide_resource_increase(self, rankings) -> bool:        \"\"\"        Decide if to increase the resources given the current rankings.        Currently we look at the rankings and if elements in the first list        have the same order also in the second list, we keep the current resource        budget. If the rankings are different, we will increase the budget.        The rankings can only be incorrect if we have rankings for both rungs.        :param rankings: list of at most two lists with tuple(trial_id, rank, score)        :return: not keep_current_budget        \"\"\"        if len(rankings) == 2:            sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung = self._get_sorted_top_rungs(rankings)        else:            return False        keep_current_budget = self._evaluate_soft_ranking(            sorted_top_rung, sorted_previous_rung        )        return not keep_current_budget    def on_task_report(self, trial_id: str, result: dict, skip_rungs: int) -> dict:        \"\"\"        Apart from calling the superclass method, we also check the rankings        and decides if to increase the current maximum resources.        \"\"\"        ret_dict = super().on_task_report(trial_id, result, skip_rungs)        if self.ranking_criterion == \"soft_ranking_auto\":            self._update_per_epoch_results(trial_id, result)            self._update_epsilon()        # check the rankings and decide if to increase the current maximum resources        rankings = self._get_top_rungs_rankings(num_rungs=2)        increase_resources = self._decide_resource_increase(rankings)        # we have a maximum amount of resources that PASHA can use        # the resources should not increase indefinitely        if increase_resources:            if self.current_rung_idx < len(self._rungs):                self.current_rung_idx += 1                # be careful, self.rung_levels is ordered with the highest resources level at the end                # moreover, since we use rung levels for counting both from the beginning and from the end of the list                # we need to remember that counting from the beginning it's zero indexed                self.current_max_t = self.rung_levels[self.current_rung_idx - 1]            else:                self.current_max_t = self.max_t        return ret_dict",
    "Experiment Result": "PASHA is an extension of ASHA that incorporates a progressive resource allocation strategy. It begins with a small initial amount of resources and gradually increases them if configuration rankings remain inconsistent within the top two rungs. The algorithm stops early when rankings stabilize. PASHA employs a 'soft ranking' mechanism, where configurations are considered equivalent if their performance difference is below a threshold epsilon (ϵ). This ϵ value can be automatically estimated by measuring noise in rankings or explicitly provided.\n\nKey configurable parameters and their behavior:\n- **ranking_criterion** (string): Determines the strategy for deciding if ranking is stable and if resources should be increased.\n  - `soft_ranking`: Uses a fixed `epsilon` threshold.\n  - `soft_ranking_std`: `epsilon` is automatically estimated from the standard deviation of scores in previous rungs, scaled by `epsilon_scaling`.\n  - `soft_ranking_median_dst` / `soft_ranking_mean_dst`: `epsilon` is estimated from the median/mean distance between scores in previous rungs, scaled by `epsilon_scaling`.\n  - `soft_ranking_auto`: Automatically estimates `epsilon` by identifying 'criss-crossing' behaviors between pairs of configurations across different resource levels (epochs), scaled by `epsilon_scaling`.\n- **epsilon** (float): Performance difference threshold for 'soft ranking'. Used directly if `ranking_criterion` is `soft_ranking`. Default is 1.0 if not specified by a command-line argument.\n- **epsilon_scaling** (float): A scaling factor applied to the automatically estimated `epsilon` when `ranking_criterion` is set to an automatic estimation method. Default is 1.0.\n- **current_rung_idx**: Internal parameter initialized to `2`, representing the index of the current top rung being considered for stabilization analysis (1-indexed).\n- **current_max_t**: The current maximum resource level that PASHA is allowed to use, dynamically adjusted based on ranking stability. Initially set to `rung_levels[current_rung_idx - 1]`.\n- **max_t** (int): A global maximum resource level that acts as a safety net to prevent indefinite resource increases.\n- **rung_levels** (list of int): Defines the specific resource levels (e.g., epochs) at which trials are evaluated and decisions are made. If not explicitly provided, these are derived from `grace_period`, `reduction_factor`, and `max_t` (e.g., in a geometric progression).\n\nPASHA requires a `metric` and `resource_attr` (e.g., 'epoch') to be reported. It can be configured as a 'promotion' type Hyperband scheduler."

    "Title": "Reshuffling Resampling Splits Can Improve Generalization of Hyperparameter Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper demonstrates that reshuffling resampling splits for every hyperparameter configuration (HPC) often improves the final model's generalization performance on unseen data, challenging the standard protocol of using fixed splits. Key contributions include a theoretical analysis explaining how reshuffling affects the asymptotic behavior of the validation loss surface and providing a bound on the expected regret. These theoretical insights are confirmed through controlled simulation studies, and the practical usefulness of reshuffling is demonstrated in large-scale, realistic HPO benchmark experiments. Notably, reshuffled holdout is shown to achieve generalization performance competitive with standard 5-fold cross-validation (CV) while being computationally cheaper.",
    "Methodology": "The methodology comprises a theoretical analysis and empirical studies. Theoretically, the paper investigates how reshuffling affects the empirical loss surface by deriving the limiting distribution of the validation loss, assuming it follows a zero-mean Gaussian process. A bound on the expected regret is derived, linking benefits of reshuffling to signal and noise characteristics. The simulation study uses a univariate quadratic loss surface combined with a noise process modeled by a squared exponential kernel, systematically varying curvature, noise correlation, and reshuffling extent. For empirical validation, benchmark experiments are conducted using Random Search, Bayesian Optimization (HEBO, SMAC3) algorithms, and various resampling strategies (holdout, M-fold CV, M-fold holdout, and their reshuffled variants). HPCs are evaluated by training on splits and validating, with splits recreated for reshuffled variants.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The simulation study involved minimizing a univariate quadratic loss surface `µ(λ) = m(λ − 0.5)^2/2` with noise `ϵ(λ)` modeled by a squared exponential kernel `K(λ, λ') = σ^2_K exp(−κ(λ − λ')^2/2)`. Parameters `τ` (reshuffling extent), `m` (curvature), and `κ` (noise correlation) were systematically varied, with each combination repeated 10,000 times. Benchmark experiments used 10 small- to medium-sized tabular datasets for binary classification from the AutoML benchmark (e.g., numerai28.6, airlines, albert). Tasks were created by sampling train-validation sets of size `n ∈ {500, 1000, 5000}`. A robust outer test set of 5000 points, distinct from HPO data, was used for generalization assessment. Learning algorithms included CatBoost, XGBoost, Elastic Net, and a funnel-shaped MLP, with detailed search spaces provided. HPO was performed using Random Search (500 HPC evaluations), HEBO (250 HPC evaluations), and SMAC3 (250 HPC evaluations). Resampling methods included holdout (80/20 train-validation split), 5-fold CV, 5-fold holdout, and 5x 5-fold CV, each with and without reshuffling. Performance metrics were ROC AUC, Accuracy, and Logloss (for Random Search) and ROC AUC (for BO). Anytime test performance was assessed by retraining the current incumbent on all available train-validation data and evaluating on the outer test set. Experiments were run for 10 replications. Total estimated compute time was 11.86 CPU years, emitting approximately 6508.67 kg CO2.",
    "Limitations": "The theoretical analysis relies on asymptotic approximations and assumptions of Gaussian loss surfaces and loss stability, which may not hold for highly sensitive losses or for dynamic HPO algorithms. Empirically, the study was restricted to tabular data and binary classification tasks, avoiding extremely small or large datasets. A specific limitation observed was that reshuffling often negatively impacted generalization when using logloss, particularly with small sample sizes.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research could explore less naive implementations of reshuffling to mitigate its negative effects on sensitive loss functions like logloss, especially with small sample sizes. Developing a unified formal definition of 'oversearching,' 'overtuning,' or 'overfitting to the validation set' in HPO and thoroughly analyzing its relationship with validation performance measurements is another avenue. Further investigation into adaptive cross-validation techniques is suggested to balance computational cost with the benefits of increased resources per HPC. Designing advanced HPO algorithms that explicitly exploit the reshuffling effect, extending the study to multi-class datasets, and combining reshuffling with existing countermeasures against overfitting (e.g., LOOCVCV, extra selection sets, early stopping) are also promising directions.",

    "Title": "Few-Shot Bayesian Optimization with Deep Kernel Surrogates",
    "Main Contributions": "This research rethinks hyperparameter optimization (HPO) as a few-shot learning problem, proposing a novel Few-Shot Bayesian Optimization (FSBO) approach. It introduces a shared deep kernel network for a Gaussian process surrogate that is meta-learned end-to-end to quickly adapt to new tasks with few evaluations. The work demonstrates state-of-the-art results in HPO for transfer learning and is the first to train probabilistic surrogate model parameters from meta-tasks via few-shot learning and fine-tune them for target tasks. Additionally, it presents an evolutionary algorithm to provide data-driven warm-start initialization for Bayesian optimization.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves adapting model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML) for Gaussian processes to construct a deep kernel network (ϕ) that learns task-independent parameters (θ, w). Training is achieved by maximizing the log marginal likelihood across all source tasks using stochastic gradient ascent (SGA). A key technique is task augmentation, where labels are randomly scaled per batch to achieve invariance to offsets and ranges, circumventing traditional label normalization issues. At test time, the learned deep kernel parameters are fine-tuned on the few available data points of the target task. Bayesian optimization uses Expected Improvement (EI) as the acquisition function. The paper also proposes an evolutionary algorithm for warm-start initialization, which finds an initial set of hyperparameters by minimizing normalized regret on source tasks, using the surrogate model's predictions, and employing mutation and crossover operations.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on three HPO problems: AdaBoost, GLMNet, and SVM. Metadata for GLMNet and SVM was sourced from OpenML, comprising 30 datasets with the most reported hyperparameter settings, while the AdaBoost dataset was publicly available. The FSBO model used a two-layer neural network (128 → 128) with ReLU activations and a squared-exponential kernel, optimized with Adam (LR 10^-3, batch size 50, warm start length 5). Methods were evaluated using leave-one-task-out cross-validation (for GLMNet/SVM) or a fixed train/test split (for AdaBoost), repeated ten times. Performance was measured by the mean of normalized regrets. Baselines included Random Search, vanilla Gaussian Process (GP with LHS or Warm Start), RGPE, MetaBO, ABLR (with and without Warm Start), and Multi-Head GPs (with and without fine-tuning). Ablation studies analyzed the contribution of warm start, fine-tuning, and the single GP. A comparison with REPTILE meta-learning was also performed.",
    "Limitations": "The paper does not explicitly list limitations of the proposed FSBO method in a dedicated section. However, it notes that the data-driven warm start initialization, while generally effective, did not provide a statistically significant advantage over Latin Hypercube Sampling for the AdaBoost problem, suggesting its benefit may vary across tasks. Most other 'limitations' discussed in the paper pertain to the scalability or performance issues of existing baseline methods, which FSBO aims to overcome.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper explicitly states that 'The use of a warm start is not the main contribution of this paper but it is interesting to explore further nonetheless,' suggesting future research into improving or further investigating warm-start initialization strategies within this few-shot HPO framework. Other potential directions, while not explicitly mentioned, could include applying FSBO to a wider range of HPO problems, exploring different meta-learning algorithms, or conducting further theoretical analysis.",

self.mf = None\n\n        normalizer = False\n        X = np.zeros((1, self.D))\n        Y = np.zeros((1, 1))\n        self.gp_is_empty = True\n        self.gp =     "Experiment Result": "The MetaBO framework adapts Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML) for Gaussian Processes (GPs) to learn a deep kernel network (ϕ) with task-independent parameters (θ, w).\n\n**Meta-Learning Training (PPO Parameters):**\n- **Optimization Algorithm:** Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO)\n- **Number of Iterations:** 2000\n- **Batch Size:** 1200\n- **Mini-Batch Size:** 60 (batch_size // 20)\n- **Number of Epochs per Iteration:** 4\n- **Learning Rate:** 1e-4 (for Adam optimizer)\n- **PPO Epsilon (Clipping Parameter):** 0.15\n- **Value Function Loss Coefficient:** 1.0\n- **Entropy Loss Coefficient:** 0.01\n- **Discount Factor (Gamma):** 0.98\n- **GAE Lambda Parameter:** 0.98\n- **Loss Type:** \"GAElam\" (Generalized Advantage Estimation)\n- **Advantage Normalization:** True\n- **Number of Parallel Workers:** 10\n- **Random Seed:** 0 (for overall PPO training and environment seeds)\n\n**Deep Kernel Network (NeuralAF Policy Architecture):**\n- **Activation Function:** ReLU (for hidden layers)\n- **Hidden Layers:** 4 layers, each with 200 units (e.g., `[200, 200, 200, 200]`)\n- **Value Network:** Used (typically with the same architecture as the policy network)\n- **Policy Input Features:** Typically includes `posterior_mean`, `posterior_std`, `timestep`, `budget`, and `x` (design variable coordinates). For some tasks like `GP-samples`, `incumbent` and `timestep_perc` are also used, and `timestep` and `budget` might be excluded from the policy network features but used in the value network.\n\n**Gaussian Process Environment Settings (Common Across Tasks):**\n- **Task Dimensionality (D):** Varies by task (e.g., 2 for Branin/GPrice/HPO, 3 for Hartmann-3, 4 for Furuta, variable for GP-samples).\n- **Function Type (`f_type`):** Defines the objective function landscape (e.g., \"BRA-var\" for Branin with variations, \"GPRICE-var\" for Goldstein-Price with variations, \"HM3-var\" for Hartmann-3 with variations, \"HPO\" for hyperparameter optimization tasks, \"Furuta\" for Furuta pendulum control, \"GP\" for Gaussian Process samples).\n- **Task Augmentation (Variations for `f_opts`):**\n    - **Translation Bounds (`bound_translation`):** 0.1 (random uniform translation applied to the objective function's domain)\n    - **Scaling Bounds (`bound_scaling`):** 0.1 (random uniform scaling applied to the objective function's output)\n    - **Number of Source Tasks/Parameters (`M`):** 50 (for Branin, GPrice, Hartmann-3, refers to a grid of fixed source tasks; for HPO and Furuta, it refers to specific dataset counts).\n- **Optimization Horizon (T):** Varies by task (e.g., 30 for Branin/GPrice/Hartmann-3, 15 for ADABOOST HPO, 20 for SVM HPO, 1-25 for Furuta, 30 for GP-samples).\n- **Initial Samples (`n_init_samples`):** 0 (no initial design is used for the MetaBO policy during training).\n- **GP Hyperparameters:** For `f_type=\"GP\"`, hyperparameters (`kernel_lengthscale`, `kernel_variance`, `noise_variance`) are sampled per task within specified ranges. For other `f_type`s (e.g., Branin, HPO), they are either fixed or determined offline via Type-2 Maximum Likelihood estimation on available data.\n- **Prior Mean Function:** Used for certain tasks (e.g., HPO, Furuta, GP-samples) but not others (e.g., Branin, GPrice, Hartmann-3).\n- **Local Acquisition Function Optimization (`local_af_opt`):** Set to True for continuous domains (e.g., Branin, GPrice, Hartmann-3, Furuta, GP-samples) and False for discrete domains (e.g., HPO). When True, multi-start optimization is used with `N_MS` (multistart grid points, e.g., 1000-10000) and `N_LS` (local search points, e.g., 1000-4000), typically selecting `k=5` best starting points.\n- **Reward Transformation (`reward_transformation`):** \"neg_log10\" is commonly used to transform simple regret into a value that is maximized, making it suitable for training (e.g., for Branin, GPrice, Hartmann-3, HPO, GP-samples). \"neg_linear\" is used for tasks where the true maximum is not known (e.g., Furuta).\n\n**Test-time Fine-tuning and Bayesian Optimization:**\n- At test time, the learned deep kernel parameters are fine-tuned on the few available data points of the target task.\n- Bayesian Optimization uses Expected Improvement (EI) as the acquisition function (implemented in `metabo.policies.policies.EI`).\n- The repository content does not include an explicit implementation of an evolutionary algorithm for warm-start initialization as described in the method."

    "Title": "Meta-learning to Improve Pre-training",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces an efficient, gradient-based algorithm to meta-learn pre-training (PT) hyperparameters, addressing the challenge of optimizing high-dimensional hyperparameters in the two-stage PT followed by fine-tuning (FT) paradigm. It formalizes \"meta-parameterized pre-training,\" where meta-parameters are incorporated into PT to improve learned representations. The proposed method obtains meta-parameter gradients by combining implicit differentiation for the longer PT stage and backpropagation through unrolled optimization for the shorter FT stage. The algorithm is shown to recover optimal meta-parameters in synthetic experiments and improves predictive performance on two real-world domains: multitask PT on protein-protein interaction graphs (up to 3.9% AUROC improvement) and self-supervised PT with SimCLR on electrocardiography data (up to 1.9% AUROC improvement).",
    "Methodology": "The methodology formalizes meta-parameterized pre-training, where meta-parameters (φ) inform the structure of the pre-training algorithm (AlgPT). The core of the method is an efficient, gradient-based algorithm to optimize these meta-parameters. For the PT best response Jacobian (∂AlgPT/∂φ), it utilizes implicit differentiation and the Implicit Function Theorem (IFT), approximating the inverse Hessian using a truncated Neumann series. For the FT best response Jacobian (∂AlgFT/∂θPT), it differentiates through a short, unrolled optimization of K steps of the fine-tuning process, as direct differentiation through convergence would yield zero gradients. The overall Algorithm 1 (detailed in Appendix B as Algorithm 2) integrates these gradient computations, performing online updates of PT and FT parameters jointly with meta-parameters. Practical considerations include handling full versus partial FT data access, splitting FT data for meta-learning (D(tr) FT and D(val) FT), and ensuring computational tractability for large models and high-dimensional meta-parameters.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The research validates its method through synthetic and real-world experiments. Synthetic experiments involved MNIST digit classification to optimize 1-D rotation augmentation meta-parameters (mean of Normal distribution) and high-dimensional per-example weighting meta-parameters (neural network weights) for PT with noisy labels. Real-world applications include: 1) Multitask pre-training for Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) on protein-protein interaction (PPI) networks, predicting 40 protein functions after PT on 5000 functions. It used a Graph Isomorphism Network (GIN) architecture, with meta-parameters weighting the 5000 PT tasks. Baselines included No PT, Graph Supervised PT, CoTrain, CoTrain + PCGrad, and CoTrain + Learned Task Weights. 2) Meta-parameterized SimCLR for semi-supervised learning (SSL) with Electrocardiogram (ECG) data (PTB-XL dataset), classifying 5 pathologies. Meta-parameters were instantiated as a neural network outputting temporal warp strength (for data augmentation: random cropping, jittering, temporal warping). A 1D CNN based on ResNet-18 was used. Baselines included No PT, SimCLR (fixed augmentation), and SimCLR + Optimized Supervised Learning Augmentations. Both real-world settings evaluated performance using AUROC under Full FT Access and Partial FT Access scenarios (limited data or unseen tasks). Experiments varied FT dataset sizes and studied the impact of K (unrolled FT steps).",
    "Limitations": "The gradient-based algorithm requires access to a model, pre-training (PT) data, and fine-tuning (FT) data (even limited FT data is sufficient, but no FT data means the method is not applicable). Meta-parameters must be differentiable. The method cannot directly optimize meta-parameters that do not affect the PT optimization landscape, such as PT learning rates. While not a significant concern in their experiments, the authors acknowledge the potential for short-horizon bias (SHB) in online optimization of certain hyperparameters, though they argue it is less impactful for hyperparameters directly affecting the optimization's fixed point.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could apply the proposed meta-learning method to optimize other potential instantiations of meta-parameters, such as learned auxiliary tasks and noise models. The societal impact statement also suggests that for applications involving healthcare data, the method should be rigorously validated in retrospective and prospective studies to detect failure modes and potential harms before deployment in real-world settings.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null
