    "Title": "Deep Ranking Ensembles for Hyperparameter Optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces a novel approach to Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) by reformulating the training of Bayesian Optimization (BO) surrogates from a regression task into a Learning-to-Rank (L2R) problem. The main contributions include: 1. A new neural network BO surrogate called Deep Ranking Ensembles (DRE), optimized with L2R losses and modeling uncertainty via ensembling. 2. A technique for meta-learning this ensemble surrogate from large-scale public meta-datasets, incorporating dataset meta-features to boost transfer quality. 3. Achieving new state-of-the-art results in HPO across a comprehensive experimental protocol involving 12 baselines, 16 HPO search spaces, and 86 datasets/tasks.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves training an ensemble of neural networks (scorers) to estimate the ranks of hyperparameter configurations' performances, rather than their direct performance values. A weighted list-wise L2R loss is used, prioritizing correctly ranking top-performing configurations. Uncertainty is modeled by training multiple diverse scorer networks within the ensemble, achieved by using different per-scorer seeds for mini-batch sampling. The method employs meta-learning to transfer knowledge from previous datasets, using a Deep Set architecture to extract dataset meta-features. These meta-features condition the scorers to adapt to new tasks, mitigating negative transfer. The meta-learning process involves updating both the scorer networks and the meta-feature network using separate query and support sets to enhance generalization. Bayesian Optimization then utilizes the posterior mean and variance of these estimated ranks with acquisition functions like Expected Improvement (EI), Lower Confidence Bound (UCB), or Average Rank to guide the search for optimal hyperparameters.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experiments were conducted on HPO-B, the largest public HPO benchmark, comprising 16 search spaces and 86 meta-test datasets, with meta-train, meta-test, and meta-validation splits. Comparisons were made against 12 state-of-the-art HPO baselines, including non-transfer methods (Random Search, GP, DNGO, BOHAMIANN, DKGP, HEBO) and transfer methods (TST, RGPE, FSBO, GCP, DKLM). The DRE architecture consisted of a DeepSet meta-feature extractor with 5 hidden layers (32 neurons each) and an ensemble of 10 MLPs, each with 4 hidden layers (32 neurons each). Meta-training involved 5000 epochs with Adam optimizer (LR 0.001, batch size 100), and fine-tuning during meta-test involved 1000 epochs per BO iteration. Experiments used 5 initial random configurations followed by 100 BO iterations. Performance was evaluated using average ranks across tasks and statistically validated with critical difference diagrams.",
    "Limitations": "The paper does not explicitly mention major limitations of the Deep Ranking Ensembles method. However, it notes that DRE incurs a higher computational cost per BO step compared to some baselines (FSBO, HEBO). This higher cost, typically less than 30 seconds, is deemed a small overhead in relation to the significantly more expensive process of evaluating hyperparameter configurations (training classifiers and computing validation accuracy).",
    "Future Research Directions": "The authors suggest that the Deep Ranking Ensembles (DRE) surrogate opens up effective avenues for improving HPO performance in various sub-problems. Specific future research directions include applying DRE to multi-fidelity HPO, multi-objective HPO, and neural architecture search.",
    "Experiment Code": "File Path: AIRBO/kernels/expected_rbf_kernel.py\nContent:\nfrom gpytorch.kernels.kernel import Kernel\nfrom typing import Optional, Tuple\nfrom gpytorch.priors import Prior\nfrom gpytorch.constraints import Interval\nimport torch\n\n\ndef postprocess_rbf(dist_mat):\n    return dist_mat.div_(-2).exp_()\n\n\ndef default_postprocess_script(x):\n    return x\n\n\nclass ExpectedRBFKernel(Kernel):\n    \"\"\"\n    Expected RBF kernel with close-form integral solution\n    \"\"\"\n    has_lengthscale = True\n\n    def __init__(self, ard_num_dims: Optional[int] = None,\n                 batch_shape: Optional[torch.Size] = torch.Size([]),\n                 active_dims: Optional[Tuple[int, ...]] = None,\n                 lengthscale_prior: Optional[Prior] = None,\n                 lengthscale_constraint: Optional[Interval] = None,\n                 eps: Optional[float] = 1e-6,\n                 **kwargs):\n        super(ExpectedRBFKernel, self).__init__(\n            ard_num_dims, batch_shape, active_dims,\n            lengthscale_prior, lengthscale_constraint, eps\n        )\n\n    def forward(self, x1, x2, diag=False, **params):\n        \"\"\"\n        We assume that x1 and x2 are Gaussian variables\n        :param x1: a tensor of M * 2D or B * M * 2D, the first D dimensions are the means and the rests are stds\n        :param x2: a tensor of N * 2D or B * M * 2D, the first D dimensions are the means and the rests are stds\n        :param diag: whether it only needs to compute the diagonal\n        :param params: configuration keywords\n        :return: a tensor of M * N\n        \"\"\"\n        B = x1.shape[:-2] if x1.ndim >= 3 else None\n        M = x1.shape[-2]\n        N = x2.shape[-2]\n        D = x1.shape[-1] // 2\n        dtype = x1.dtype\n        device = x1.device\n\n        x1_mean = x1[..., 0:D]\n        x1_var = x1[..., D:] ** 2.0\n        x2_mean = x2[..., 0:D]\n        x2_var = x2[..., D:] ** 2.0\n\n        Var1 = torch.diag_embed(x1_var)  # B * M * D * D\n        Var2 = torch.diag_embed(x2_var)  # B * N * D * D\n\n        if self.ard_num_dims is None:\n            ls_vec = self.lengthscale.repeat(1, D).view(-1)\n        else:\n            ls_vec = self.lengthscale.view(-1)\n        W = torch.diag(ls_vec ** 2.0)  # lengthscale in (D, D)\n\n        # numerator\n        AB = x1_mean.unsqueeze(-2) - x2_mean.unsqueeze(-3)  # AB=A-B: broadcast --> (B, M, N, D)\n        VAVB = Var1.unsqueeze(-3) + Var2.unsqueeze(-4)  # VAVB = VarA + VarB: (M, N, D, D)\n        Z = W.unsqueeze(0).unsqueeze(0) if B is None \\\n            else W.unsqueeze(0).unsqueeze(0).unsqueeze(\n            0) + VAVB  # Z = W + VarA + VarB: (M, N, D, D)\n        Z_inv = torch.inverse(Z)  # (M, N, D, D)\n\n        ABZ_eq = 'mnpd,mndd->mnpd' if B is None else 'bmnpd,bmndd->bmnpd'\n        ABZ = torch.einsum(ABZ_eq, [AB.unsqueeze(-2), Z_inv])  # (A-B)Z^-1, (M, N, 1, D)\n        nu_eq = 'mnpd,mndq->mnpq' if B is None else 'bmnpd,bmndq->bmnpq'\n        nu = torch.einsum(nu_eq, [ABZ, AB.unsqueeze(-1)]).squeeze(-1).squeeze(\n            -1)  # (A-B)Z^-1(A-B)^T:(M, N, 1, 1)\n\n        # denominator\n        x1_eq_x2 = torch.equal(x1, x2)\n        if diag:\n            # Special case the diagonal because we can return all zeros most of the time.\n            if x1_eq_x2:\n                res = torch.zeros(*x1.shape[:-1], x1.shape[-2], dtype=x1.dtype, device=x1.device)\n                res = postprocess_rbf(res)\n                return res\n            else:\n                res = torch.norm(x1 - x2, p=2, dim=-1)\n                res = res.pow(2)\n                res = postprocess_rbf(res)\n                return res\n        else:\n            W_inv = W.inverse().unsqueeze(0).unsqueeze(0)\n            if B is not None:\n                W_inv = W_inv.unsqueeze(0)\n            de_coeff = (W_inv.matmul(VAVB)).det().sqrt()\n            if x1_eq_x2:\n                # if the x1 == x2, then it needs separate computations for the diagonal\n                # and non-diagonal elements, see the paper appendix C for the details.\n                zero_diag_identity = (torch.ones((M, M), dtype=dtype).to(device) -\n                                      torch.eye(M, M, dtype=dtype).to(device))\n                one_diag = torch.eye(M, M, dtype=dtype).to(device)\n                if B is not None:\n                    zero_diag_identity = zero_diag_identity.unsqueeze(0)\n                    one_diag = one_diag.unsqueeze(0)\n                de = de_coeff * zero_diag_identity + one_diag\n            else:\n                de = de_coeff\n\n            covar = nu / de\n\n            return postprocess_rbf(covar)\n\nFile Path: AIRBO/kernels/kme_kernel.py\nContent:\n\"\"\"\nKernel-mean-embedding Kernel defined in uGP-UCB:\n    K(X, X`) = \\int_x \\int_x` k(x, x`) dP(x) dP(x`)\n\"\"\"\nfrom gpytorch.kernels.kernel import Kernel\nimport gc\nfrom utils import commons as cm\nfrom functools import partial\nimport torch\n\n\ndef postprocess_linear(dist_mat):\n    sym_dist_mat = ((dist_mat + dist_mat.transpose(-2, -1)) * 0.5)\n    return sym_dist_mat\n\n\ndef integral_KME_batch(X, Y, kernel: Kernel):\n    \"\"\"\n    Estimate the KME via sampling and integral\n    :param X: B * M * C * D tensor, B is the batch size, M is the sample size,\n              C is the sampling size, and D is the data dim.\n    :param Y: B * N * H * D tensor, B is the batch size, N is the sample size,\n              H is the sampling size, and D is the data dim.\n    :param kernel: A gpytorch.kernel.Kernel instance\n    :return: a tensor of M * N\n    \"\"\"\n    dist_mat = None\n    try:\n        B = X.shape[:-3]\n        M, C, D = X.shape[-3:]\n        N, H, D = Y.shape[-3:]\n        _original_shape = kernel.batch_shape\n        kernel.batch_shape = torch.Size([M, N])\n        dist_mat = kernel(\n            X.unsqueeze(-3),  # cast into B * M * 1 * m * D\n            Y.unsqueeze(-4)  # cast into B * 1 * N * m * D\n        ).evaluate()  # B * M * N * m * m\n\n        kme = dist_mat.mean(dim=[-1, -2])\n        kernel.batch_shape = _original_shape\n    finally:\n        cm.free_memory(\n            [\n                dist_mat,\n            ],\n            debug=False\n        )\n\n    return kme\n\n\ndef estimate_KME_in_chunks(X, Y, estimator, chunk_size=10):\n    \"\"\"\n    Estimate the KME in chunks, decrease the chunk size if GPU memory error happens\n    :param X: B * M * C * D or M * C * D tensor\n    :param Y: B * N * H * D or N * H * D tensor\n    :param estimator:\n    :param chunk_size:\n    :return:\n    \"\"\"\n    N = Y.shape[-3]\n\n    retry_i = 0\n    success = False\n    _practicable_chunk_size = chunk_size\n    kme_results = None\n    while not success:\n        kme_results = []\n        _Y = None\n        _kme = None\n        try:\n            _practicable_chunk_size = max(chunk_size // (2 ** retry_i), 1)\n            for bs in range(0, N, _practicable_chunk_size):\n                be = min(N, bs + _practicable_chunk_size)\n                _Y = Y[..., bs:be, :, :]\n                _kme = estimator(X, _Y)\n                kme_results.append(_kme)\n\n                gc.collect()\n                torch.cuda.empty_cache()\n            success = True\n        except RuntimeError as e:\n            if 'CUDA out of memory' in e.args[0] or 'not enough memory' in e.args[0]:\n                if _practicable_chunk_size > 1:  # we can still try to reduce the chunk size\n                    print(f'Chunk size {_practicable_chunk_size} is too large, '\n                          f'reduce it by a half:', e)\n                    retry_i += 1\n\n                    if len(kme_results) > 0:\n                        cm.free_memory(kme_results)\n                        for _m in kme_results:\n                            del _m\n                        del kme_results\n                    if '_kme' in locals():\n                        cm.free_memory([_kme])\n                    if '_Y' in locals():\n                        cm.free_memory([_Y])\n                else:\n                    raise ValueError('Chunk size has been reduced to 1 but still out of memory, '\n                                     'try cpu.')\n            else:\n                raise e\n        finally:\n            gc.collect()\n            torch.cuda.empty_cache()\n\n    kme = torch.concat(kme_results, dim=-1)  # concat to columns as we chuck the Y\n    return kme\n\n\nclass KMEKernel(Kernel):\n    \"\"\"\n    Decorating an existing kernel with KME\n    \"\"\"\n\n    has_lengthscale = True\n\n    def __init__(self, base_kernel, **kwargs):\n        super(KMEKernel, self).__init__(**kwargs)\n        self.base_kernel = base_kernel\n        self.chunk_size = kwargs.get('chunk_size', 100)\n        self.estimator = kwargs.get('estimator', 'integral')\n        self.estimation_trials = kwargs.get('estimation_trials', 1)\n        if self.estimator == 'integral':\n            self.estimation_func = partial(\n estimate the MMD\n    :param X: B*D tensor\n    :param Y: H*D tensor\n    :param kernel: gpytorch.kernels.Kernel\n    :return: a scalar\n    \"\"\"\n    # xx\n    cm_xx = kernel(X, X).evaluate()\n error happens\n    :param X: B * M * C * D or M * C * D tensor\n    :param Y: B * N * H * D or N * H * D tensor\n    :param estimator:\n    :param chunk_size:\n    :return:\n    \"\"\"\n    N = empirical_mmd_batch(X, Y, kernel: gpyt.kernels.Kernel):\n    \"\"\"\n    Empirically estimate the MMD in batches\n    :param X: M * B * D tensor, M is the batch size, B is the sample size, D is the def compute_mean_cov(self, x, **kwargs):\n        \"\"\"\n        compute the mean and covariance matrix\n        :param x: a tuple of (Xc_raw, Xc_samples, Xe),\n        where Xc_raw is raw inputs, gpyt.settings.lazily_evaluate_kernels(False):\n                k_c = self.covar_module(proj_X_samples, **kwargs)\n            covar = k_c if covar is None else (k_c * covar)\n\n            # mean\n 0:\n            Xe_trans = self.xe_transformer(Xe)\n            k_e = self.xe_covar_module(Xe_trans, **kwargs)\n            covar = k_e if covar is None else (k_e * covar)\n\n        return mean_x, covar\n\n    def define_additional_transform(self, **kwargs):\n        xc_sample_size = kwargs.get('xc_sample_size', 1000)\n        input_sampling_func = kwargs['input_sampling_func']\n        n_var = Concatenate the input to the training input\n            full_inputs = []\n            batch_shape = train_inputs[0].shape[:-2]\n            for i, (train_input, input) in enumerate(zip(train_inputs, inputs)):\n                # Make sure the batch shapes agree for training/test data\n                # special operations for MMD kernel\n                dim_2_concat = -3 if i == 1 else -2\n                batch_reserved_dim = -3 if i == 1 else -2\n                batch_shape = train_inputs[i].shape[:batch_reserved_dim]\n                if batch_shape != train_input.shape[:batch_reserved_dim]:\n                    batch_shape = torch.broadcast_shapes(batch_shape,\n                                                         train_input.shape[:batch_reserved_dim])\n                    train_input = train_input.expand(*batch_shape,\n                                                     *train_input.shape[batch_reserved_dim:])\n                if batch_shape != input.shape[:batch_reserved_dim]:\n                    batch_shape = torch.broadcast_shapes(batch_shape,\n                                                         input.shape[:batch_reserved_dim])\n                    train_input = train_input.expand(*batch_shape,\n                                                     *train_input.shape[batch_reserved_dim:])\n                    input = input.expand(*batch_shape, *input.shape[batch_reserved_dim:])\n                full_inputs.append(torch.cat([train_input, input], dim=dim_2_concat))\n\n            #     "Experiment Result": "The experimental setup focuses on Robust Bayesian Optimization (BO) using Gaussian Process (GP) models designed to handle uncertain inputs. The core components and their configurations are as follows:\n\n**1. GP Model Architectures:**\n*   **MMDGP (Maximum Mean Discrepancy GP):** Uses an `MMDKernel` for covariance and an optional `MLP` (`latent_mapping_module`) to project raw inputs (e.g., hyperparameter configurations) to a latent space. It is designed to work with inputs represented by samples.\n*   **UGP (Uncertain GP with KME):** Inherits from `MMDGP` but replaces the covariance module with a `KMEKernel`.\n*   **UncertainGP (Uncertain GP with explicit std/var):** Employs kernels like `ExpectedRBFKernel` (ERBF), `GaussianSymmetrizedKLKernel` (SKL), or a standard `RBFKernel`. It is designed to directly handle inputs given as means and standard deviations.\n*   **RobustGP:** A base class for these GP models, providing common functionalities for input and outcome transformations.\n\n**2. Handling Input Uncertainty:**\n*   **For MMDGP and UGP:** Inputs (`xc_raw`) are augmented with `xc_samp` (multiple samples drawn around the raw input configuration), corresponding to `INPUT_TYPE_SAMPLES` in `model_common_utils.py`. The `AdditionalFeatures` transform with `additional_xc_samples` is used for this purpose.\n*   **For UncertainGP:** Inputs are represented by their mean (`xc_raw`) and standard deviation (`xc_std`), corresponding to `INPUT_TYPE_DISTRIBUTION`. The `AdditionalFeatures` transform with `additional_std` is used to generate the standard deviation tensor.\n\n**3. Custom Kernels for Uncertainty:**\n*   **MMDKernel:** Calculates Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) between distributions represented by samples. It supports `nystrom` and `empirical` estimators for MMD. `xc_sample_size` (e.g., 160) and `sub_samp_size` (e.g., 10) are configured for these estimators.\n*   **KMEKernel:** Implements the Kernel Mean Embedding (KME) to compute similarity between distributions. It uses an `integral` estimator for its calculation.\n*   **ExpectedRBFKernel (ERBF):** A specialized RBF kernel that expects inputs to be Gaussian distributions (mean and variance). It calculates the kernel function by integrating over these input distributions.\n*   **GaussianSymmetrizedKLKernel (SKL):** Another kernel for comparing Gaussian input distributions, utilizing the symmetric Kullback-Leibler divergence.\n\n**4. Optimization (Bayesian Optimization Context):**\n*   **Acquisition Functions:** `ExpectedImprovement (EI)` and `UpperConfidenceBound (UCB)` are employed to guide the search for optimal hyperparameters based on the posterior mean and variance provided by the GP models.\n*   **BO Loop Parameters:** The optimization runs for `n_iter` iterations (e.g., 100). Each iteration suggests `batch_size` candidates (e.g., 1). Acquisition function optimization itself involves `n_restarts` (e.g., 10) and draws `raw_samples` (e.g., 512) to find the best next candidate.\n*   **GP Model Training:** Models are trained for `epoch_num` epochs (e.g., 150) with a `learning_rate (lr)` (e.g., 5e-2). Training can optionally use `scipy` for optimization or a custom Adam optimizer. `fit_restarts` (e.g., 3) are configured to handle potential `NotPSDError` during GP fitting.\n*   **Evaluation:** The true expectation of objective values for a candidate is estimated by drawing `num_expectation_eval` samples (e.g., 700) from its input uncertainty distribution and evaluating the objective function on these samples."
}{
    "Title": "Meta-Learning Adversarial Bandit Algorithms",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces the first formal guarantees for online meta-learning in the adversarial online-within-online setting with bandit feedback. It designs meta-algorithms (Algorithm 1) to tune initialization and hyperparameters of Online Mirror Descent (OMD) for Multi-Armed Bandits (MAB) and Bandit Linear Optimization (BLO). For MAB, it meta-learns Tsallis-entropic OMD, bounding task-averaged regret by the entropy of optimal arms, and achieves O(sqrt(sm log d)) regret for s-sparse optimal arms, outperforming minimax rates in specific cases. For BLO, it uses OMD with self-concordant barrier regularizers, yielding task-similarity measures tied to action space constraints. The approach is adaptive, not requiring prior knowledge of task similarity.",
    "Methodology": "The meta-algorithm (Algorithm 1) combines three full-information online learning algorithms: Follow-the-Leader (FTL) for updating OMD's initialization, Exponentially Weighted Online Optimization (EWOO) for tuning the step-size, and Multiplicative Weights (MW) for selecting regularizer-specific parameters (beta for MAB, epsilon for BLO) from a discretized grid. The core technique involves optimizing a sequence of affine functions that upper-bound OMD's regret. For MAB, it employs Tsallis entropy as a regularizer, utilizing both implicit exploration (low-variance loss estimators) and guaranteed exploration (unbiased estimators with a gap assumption for best-arm identification). For BLO, self-concordant barrier functions are used as regularizers to adapt to various convex domains.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The paper is theoretical, focusing on designing algorithms with formal regret guarantees. It does not describe empirical experimental setups, datasets, or benchmarks in the traditional sense. Validation is achieved through mathematical proofs and derivations of regret bounds under adversarial online-within-online settings for Multi-Armed Bandits and Bandit Linear Optimization problems. Specific assumptions, such as a non-zero performance gap (Delta > 0) between best and second-best arms for some MAB results, are part of the theoretical framework rather than experimental conditions.",
    "Limitations": "Key limitations include the strong assumption of a nonzero performance gap (Delta > 0) between the best and second-best arms for MAB to adapt to the entropy of true optima, which is difficult to avoid. For MAB with guaranteed exploration, results contain 'fast terms' meaning task-averaged regret may not converge to zero even for identical tasks. The meta-tuning process can be computationally costly, requiring multiple single-dimensional integrals per task, leading to slower regret rates in T compared to single-task or simpler meta-learning algorithms. For some BLO applications (e.g., shortest path), an explicit, easily interpretable task-similarity measure linked to the boundary offset epsilon is not clearly stated.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future work could focus on overcoming the current limitations, such as adapting to a notion of task-similarity that depends on the true optima for MAB without assuming a gap, and developing similar adaptations for BLO. Another direction is to design meta-learning algorithms that offer 'best-of-both-worlds' guarantees, adapting dynamically to both stochastic and adversarial bandit environments. Additionally, the framework could be extended to other partial information settings, including contextual bandits and bandit convex optimization problems.",
